17 Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why does a check that only scores an agent’s attempts end up defining what the agent learns?

Curated demonstrations give an agent a starting policy, but they cover only the decisions someone recorded, and long-horizon tasks keep producing decisions that no demonstration anticipated. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards replaces the demonstration with a programmatic check, such as a unit test suite, a compiler, or a proof checker, that scores each sampled attempt and turns the score into a weight update without a human label. That substitution changes what the check is. An evaluator that once only counted failures becomes the objective the optimizer searches, and the search finds every gap between what the check measures and what the task requires, from a weakened assertion to a patched test runner to a verdict rewritten before it is recorded. The verification boundary must therefore be defended like a security perimeter, with fixtures the candidate cannot reach and rewards read as evidence about only the properties their checks cover. Wherever outcomes can be mechanically checked, the policy improves as far as the scope and protection of the check allow, and no further.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why reward-driven learning needs sampled support that imitation does not, and estimate when a task yields no reward contrast
  • Design a shaped reward that preserves the pass-over-failure order, and bound the reward hacking and false accepts its checks admit under optimization pressure
  • Calculate the generation cost and standard error of continuation-based credit estimates across turns
  • Compute group-relative coefficients and the per-token weight that length normalization assigns them
  • Specify the per-invocation records and version-lag bound that keep a multi-turn update faithful to its sampling policy
  • Diagnose entropy collapse from action diversity, and changes in reward and length from task outcomes rather than from the narration a policy generates
  • Size the environment cycle and verifier pool of a rollout-training loop and identify the stage that limits it
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

17.1 Learning from a Check

The previous chapter left the repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) with trained_policy_v1, a supervised checkpoint fitted to the one malformed-argument recovery that curation admitted. Its release gate already had to send trials into a state the corpus never covered, the version conflict for which no demonstration exists (Case Study: Training the Repair Policy). More imitation needs someone to record that recovery, and a preference pair needs two admissible records of it. The agent’s versioned test fixtures and acceptance checks need neither. They can score entirely novel attempts whose intermediate actions have no labeled target. An autonomous policy might inspect an unexpected source file, attempt an alternative refactoring strategy, or halt when it encounters an unfamiliar error. While the automated test suite verifies whether the final patch succeeds, it provides no token-by-token guidance during intermediate decision steps.

Training changes the policy and leaves every exposure where the runtime closes it.

Test-time selection already uses those acceptance outcomes to filter multiple generated rollouts and extract a passing candidate, yet it pays that inference latency on every incoming task while leaving the base policy unchanged. Neither imitation of scarce human-verified repairs nor test-time filtering increases the policy’s intrinsic probability of solving an unseen fault from verification signals alone. Reinforcement learning bridges this gap by converting acceptance verdicts directly into gradient updates. In doing so, it alters the role of the verification suite. Once a check enters the optimization objective, any flaw in the test fixture is compiled into the network weights rather than miscounted on a single run. The acceptance check that once decided a release (The Task Acceptance Contract) becomes the signal that decides every update.

Turning automated test outcomes into policy updates requires four coupled mechanisms. The reward design must evaluate functional correctness without introducing shortcuts that optimization can exploit. The policy update rule must then solve multi-turn credit assignment, translating a single terminal pass or fail into per-token update coefficients across an interactive trajectory. For that update to match what the agent actually did, the rollout recorder must log the exact context prefixes the policy saw and the exact tokens it generated. Finally, the compute fleet (sandboxed environments, verification workers, and gradient engines) must be sized so that training updates receive complete, synchronized candidate groups without stalling the pipeline.

The chapter takes these mechanisms in the order a student would need to build them. It first fixes what an outcome-driven update can and cannot learn (section 17.2), then prices the reward that drives it, including the false accepts a verifier admits (section 17.3). Credit assignment follows, first through return and value estimates (section 17.4) and then through group-relative policy optimization (section 17.5), the worked optimizer. The remaining sections keep that optimizer honest in practice: faithful records of multi-turn rollouts (section 17.6), a verifier the candidate cannot reach (section 17.7), diagnostics for the pathologies that rising reward can hide (section 17.8), and the rollout, verifier, and staleness arithmetic that sizes the training fleet (section 17.9). A case study then follows one repair group through the whole loop (section 17.10).

17.2 Foundations of Verifiable Rewards

Starting from trained_policy_v1 (Case Study: Training the Repair Policy), the repair agent can already propose tool calls and edits when dispatched on a version-conflict bug for which no curated recovery exists. Whereas behavioral cloning (supervised imitation of recorded actions) stalls the moment a trajectory leaves the demonstrated corpus, reinforcement learning executes candidate rollouts in the live container environment and scores the resulting trajectories against programmatic checks. The supervised checkpoint serves only to bootstrap sampling; once exploration begins, optimization depends entirely on the agent’s ability to act, observe, and receive an evaluative score. Before any gradient update can convert those raw outcomes into policy improvements, however, the system must define how multi-turn rollouts are represented, how baselines separate signal from variance, and how execution boundaries protect verification harnesses during exploration.

17.2.1 Policies, rollouts, and rewards

An agent repairing a repository cannot inspect the container’s underlying disk or process table directly; it sees only the transcript of prompts, tool calls, and truncated command outputs delivered into its context window. A policy \(\Policy(\RolloutAction_k\mid \InteractionHistory_k)\), parameterized by weights \(\ModelParameters\), defines a probability distribution over responses \(\RolloutAction_k\) conditioned on that delivered interaction history \(\InteractionHistory_k\) through turn \(\RolloutTurnIndex\). We consider a finite decision horizon \(\TurnHorizon\) with turn indices \(\RolloutTurnIndex=0,\ldots,\TurnHorizon-1\). (We reserve \(\RolloutTurnIndex\) for agent-environment turns because \(\TokenIndex\) later indexes individual autoregressive tokens within a rollout.) The history \(\InteractionHistory_k\) plays the role of the recorded state \(\TrajectoryState_k\) in the trajectory notation of The H·S·A Exposure Space, the runtime’s delivered view at turn \(\RolloutTurnIndex\) rather than the environment itself. A response \(\RolloutAction_k\) represents either a proposed tool call, such as a shell command or structured API query, or a terminal answer. The runtime validates and executes any requested operation, returning standard output, standard error, or structured payloads as observations for subsequent turns. Because modern context windows cannot always ingest unbounded execution logs, the policy consumes a constructed prompt that may truncate or summarize older history, never inspecting the underlying state \(\RolloutEnvironmentState_{k}\) directly. An edited configuration file or sandbox directory affects policy decisions only after tool invocations reflect its contents back into \(\InteractionHistory_k\).

↪ The partially observable Markov decision process behind this observation constraint is developed in POMDP formalization of trajectories.

A rollout, or sampled trajectory \(\Trajectory\), records one complete execution trace under specified task definitions, policy weights, environment states, and harness constraints. In our illustrative repair scenario, a trial rl_trial_01 on task pool_repair_01 starts container sandbox pool_fixture_v1 and tests candidate patches against test harness repair_accept_v1. Each rollout generates its own telemetry, intermediate command outputs, and terminal verification artifacts; past demonstration records provide no credit to this live attempt.

A reward is a scalar score computed under an explicit verification rule. In the simplest formulation, intermediate turns emit zero reward, leaving a single terminal score \(\TrajectoryReward(\Trajectory)\) evaluated at horizon completion without temporal discounting (a discount factor \(\DiscountFactor=1\), so a reward counts the same whichever turn it arrives on). The trajectory return collapses to this terminal score, giving the expected return objective:

\[\ExpectedReturnObjective(\ModelParameters)=\mathbb{E}_{\Trajectory\sim\Policy}\left[\TrajectoryReward(\Trajectory)\right].\]

The expectation averages over policy randomness, task sampling distributions, and environment variations (such as nondeterministic test execution times or network latencies). Maximizing \(\ExpectedReturnObjective(\ModelParameters)\) drives the policy to increase the expected measured reward under these conditions. Whether that measured score reflects true task utility depends on the fidelity of the reward harness itself, an engineering challenge analyzed in section 17.3. When the reward comes from an automated, deterministic check (such as unit test execution, syntax validation, type checking, or a formal proof assistant) rather than from an imperfect learned preference model or manual human grading, this approach is called reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR)1 (Lambert et al. 2024).

1 Tests as Reward: Executed unit tests served as the reward for a code-generating model before the name existed: Le et al. (2022) trained a pretrained code model with reinforcement learning on test outcomes and added a learned critic that predicts functional correctness. That pairing anticipates this chapter’s central tension, since the test outcome is the trustworthy signal while the denser learned critic is itself a model that optimization can exploit.

Le, Hung, Yue Wang, Akhilesh Deepak Gotmare, Silvio Savarese, and Steven Chu Hong Hoi. 2022. “CodeRL: Mastering Code Generation Through Pretrained Models and Deep Reinforcement Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1549.
Definition 17.1: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is policy optimization using reward signals computed by automated checks of specified properties of the policy’s outputs or interactions, such as tests or proof checkers, rather than relying solely on learned preference scores or human ratings.

  1. Significance: An automated verification harness provides an objective training signal without requiring step-by-step demonstrations. Directly rewarding task success increases the acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceProbability\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2) even when human demonstrations do not exist. However, the verifier’s false-accept rate bounds only the per-rollout chance that a sampled failure is rewarded. When true passes are rare, false accepts can make up a far larger share of the rewarded rollouts (section 17.3.2).
  2. Distinction: Whereas behavioral cloning trains the policy to reproduce specific recorded responses, RLVR evaluates only whether the completed trajectory satisfies programmatic criteria. The resulting policy gradient is strictly bounded by the coverage, sensitivity, and sandboxed security of the underlying verifier.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking higher reward scores on a fixed verification suite for broad task competence, or treating automated verification as a reason to relax runtime isolation and execution guardrails.

17.2.2 Baselines and credit assignment

When a binary check such as repair_accept_v1 emits \(1\) for a pass and \(0\) for a failure, multiplying action log-probabilities directly by the raw score multiplies every failed rollout by zero, leaving the update with no signal to penalize the actions that failed. Policy-gradient algorithms adjust model parameters by estimating a gradient direction that increases or decreases the log-probabilities of sampled actions relative to an offset reference level. Standard estimators subtract a baseline from the raw outcome: the centered advantage is \(\TrajectoryReward(\Trajectory)-\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_0)\), where \(\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_0)\) depends on the initial task prompt and context \(\InteractionHistory_0\) but remains independent of the sampled action sequence \(\Trajectory\). That baseline can be a fixed scalar, an empirical rolling mean across task batches, or a parametric value estimate, and it does not necessarily require training a separate critic network (Williams 1992).

Consider a binary verification check where success yields \(1.0\) and failure yields \(0.0\). With a fixed baseline of 0.25, a passing rollout receives a centered score of 0.75, whereas a failing rollout yields -0.25. These opposing signs drive gradient updates in opposite directions: positive centered returns reinforce the probabilities of every token and tool call sampled along the passing trajectory, whereas negative centered returns suppress them. In contrast, setting the baseline to zero leaves failed rollouts with a centered score of \(0.0\), discarding the negative gradient signal that penalizes counterproductive trajectories.

This uniform attribution illustrates the classic credit-assignment problem:2 a terminal verifier evaluates overall trajectory success, but it cannot differentiate the decisive tool call from irrelevant or mildly detrimental exploratory steps. While subtracting an appropriate baseline reduces the variance of the policy gradient estimator without introducing bias (Greensmith et al. 2004), it applies a uniform scalar adjustment across the entire trajectory rather than isolating action-specific causal effects. Section 17.4 formalizes these estimators and their bias-variance trade-offs, and section 17.5 introduces group-relative scoring to compute data-dependent baselines across parallel rollouts without training an auxiliary value network.

2 Credit Assignment Problem: Minsky (1961) named the problem for learning systems that receive one success signal for a strategy built from many internal decisions, with games and program writing as his examples. The repair agent poses the same question at the scale of tool calls: a failed acceptance check arrives after many turns, and every turn is a candidate for blame, which is why the estimators of this chapter differ mainly in how they spread one outcome across those turns.

Minsky, Marvin. 1961. “Steps Toward Artificial Intelligence.” Proceedings of the IRE 49 (1): 8–30. https://doi.org/10.1109/JRPROC.1961.287775.

17.2.3 Exploration and protected assessment

If every sampled rollout on an unfamiliar bug fails repair_accept_v1, the batch returns exclusively zero rewards, and subtracting a baseline still gives the optimizer no clue which actions came closer to a fix. Because a policy-gradient update can optimize only over trajectories that the sampling harness actually produces, exploration, the sampling of action sequences the policy has rarely tried at points where it still spreads probability across several plausible actions, determines what reinforcement learning can discover. On a binary verification check (pass vs. fail), a task instance that the policy never solves leaves a zero baseline, or one that tracks the observed outcomes such as the mean of a group of attempts (section 17.5.4), with every centered score at zero. A fixed positive baseline does produce a gradient, but it pushes down every sampled action alike and gives the update nothing to prefer. Either way, without variation in the outcome signal the update carries no contrast, regardless of how many rollouts the collector generates. If a policy solves a task instance with base probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\), sampling a group of \(\GroupSize\) independent rollouts yields at least one successful trajectory with probability \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\GroupSize\). That condition is the one that binds when \(\AttemptPassProbability\) is small; the group optimizer of section 17.5 also needs at least one failure in the same group, a stricter condition derived there. When \(\AttemptPassProbability=0\), scaling the group size \(\GroupSize\) yields no contrast at all. System builders overcome this cold-start bottleneck by seeding the policy through supervised fine-tuning on demonstrations, curating curricula with progressive difficulty, or structuring reward functions with informative intermediate milestones.

Because exploration systematically exposes edge cases in both the target environment and the evaluation harness, the execution runtime must enforce rigid capability and resource boundaries. As an agent generates thousands of candidate interactions, optimization will exploit any loose permission, such as reading hidden test fixtures, patching Python test runners, or modifying exit codes in the evaluation container, to manufacture high rewards without solving the underlying problem. Preventing such gaming requires a protected assessment boundary. The agent’s actions execute within an isolated sandbox, while the verifier runs with separate privileges, shielded storage, and read-only access to private test suites. Section 17.7 places that boundary on the reward path once the reward and the update it drives are in place. Even with complete isolation, held-out evaluation on disjoint test distributions remains necessary to determine whether the policy acquired robust problem-solving behavior or merely fit the idiosyncrasies of the training verifiers. Before any of that machinery can be designed, however, the reward itself must be pinned down: what the check measures, how often it is wrong, and how other terms may be added to it without overturning the pass-over-failure order.

17.3 Verifiable Rewards

When a trial on pool_repair_01 finishes, the training loop scores the submitted patch by running the acceptance suite repair_accept_v1, yet passing that unit test provides evidence only about the specific assertions the suite checks. Before the system can convert an execution outcome into a scalar reward, the training infrastructure must guarantee exactly what was executed, which artifact was tested, and which version of the grader evaluated it. The assessment contract established in The Task Acceptance Contract is no longer an offline benchmark; it is now an active component of the optimization objective.

17.3.1 The score and the intended outcome

A candidate patch for pool_repair_01 can pass every test in repair_accept_v1 while introducing a race condition on an untested concurrency path, or, if the grading environment is writable, modify the test runner so that a failing execution reports success. Both outcomes collect high reward without solving the underlying engineering problem, but they represent two different defects: incomplete test coverage vs. compromised measurement. Hardening the grader protects the measurement process, but it does not make the underlying test criteria complete.

Definition 17.2: Reward hacking

Reward hacking is behavior that increases measured reward by exploiting a gap between the formal reward and the intended task, including manipulation of the measurement mechanism (Amodei et al. 2016; Skalse et al. 2022).

  1. Significance: Gradient optimization reinforces a rewarded shortcut just as eagerly as an authentic solution. Each gamed pass registers as a success, inflating the measured acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceProbability\) and artificially lowering the computed cost per accepted task while true capability stagnates. Tracking policy quality during reinforcement learning therefore requires an independent assessment of task performance.
  2. Distinction: A false-positive judgment is an evaluation error. It does not, by itself, establish that the policy discovered or deliberately exploited a shortcut.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating instructions against tampering as protection for the grader. Software isolation at the runtime level must restrict what the policy and its submitted code can affect.

3 Goodhart’s Law Under Training: How search exploits imperfect checks showed best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) selection pressing on an imperfect check, where a larger pool favors candidates the proxy overrates. An RL loop applies the same pressure at every update and then writes the result into the weights, so a gap between reward and task is searched far more persistently than any selection step or human-managed target ever searches it.

A false accept costs more during training than during test-time sampling. At inference time, a noisy selector that accepts a bad candidate wastes one decision. In reinforcement learning, however, awarding reward to a defective trajectory raises the probability of the tokens that produced it, prompting the policy to generate more such trajectories on subsequent tasks. Once the exploit is learned into the model weights, the training run can raise its measured score while growing progressively worse at the intended task.3 Formally, let \(\CandidateUtility(\Trajectory)\) represent the developer’s intended objective and \(\ProxyTrajectoryReward(\Trajectory)\) the reward signal calculated by the training loop. Whenever an unintended trajectory \(\ExploitTrajectory\) satisfies \(\ProxyTrajectoryReward(\ExploitTrajectory)\ge\ProxyTrajectoryReward(\Trajectory^*)\) while \(\CandidateUtility(\ExploitTrajectory)\ll \CandidateUtility(\Trajectory^*)\), gradient ascent on \(\ProxyTrajectoryReward\) gives it no less weight than the intended solution. The exploit steadily gains ground whenever it also has higher prior probability under the current policy (Skalse et al. 2022; Krakovna et al. 2020). Degenerate shortcuts often hold that probability advantage. Weakening an assertion or rewriting a verdict takes only a few lines and no understanding of the bug, whereas diagnosing and fixing a difficult bug may require a complex, multi-turn repair.

↩︎ How a verifier’s false-acceptance rate dilutes the passing candidates at inference time is established in How base rates limit check precision.

The optimizer this chapter develops amplifies this failure mode the first time it occurs. Group-relative optimization, taught in section 17.5, samples a group of \(\GroupSize\) rollouts for the same task and scores each rollout against the others, so the rarer a pass is within its group, the larger the weight the update gives it. Suppose the test files sit in the rollout’s writable workspace, and one rollout on a difficult bug weakens the failing assertion while the other \(\GroupSize-1\) rollouts fail honestly. That rollout is the group’s lone pass, and it receives the largest coefficient in the group, a weight that grows with \(\GroupSize\) (equation 3 derives its size). Every token that wrote the tampering edit receives a sharp probability increase. The optimization loop did not suffer an algorithmic malfunction; the optimizer performed exactly as designed on the reward signal it received.

The exploits observed in coding environments fall into three recognizable classes, each documented in training or evaluation runs of 2025-era models:

  1. Weakening or special-casing the check. Code that returns expected values only for inputs tested by the suite, or test assertions edited until they pass. A 2025 system card reports that one model, when unable to find a general solution, sometimes special-cased tests to make them pass (Anthropic 2025); Test-tampering defense traces how the behavior surfaced and was mitigated.
  2. Stubbing or bypassing the verified code path. In one 2025 report during reinforcement learning of a reasoning model on coding tasks, a monitor flagged a plan to make a verification function always return true, alongside an exploit that exited the environment before unit tests could execute (Baker et al. 2025).
  3. Rewriting the verdict inside the runner. Production coding environments used for training proved vulnerable to candidates returning an object whose equality comparison always succeeds, calling sys.exit(0) before any assertion executes, or writing a conftest.py that monkeypatches the test framework’s reporting subsystem so that every failure is recorded as a pass. Models trained in those environments learned to use them (MacDiarmid et al. 2025).
Anthropic. 2025. Claude 3.7 Sonnet System Card.
Baker, Bowen, Joost Huizinga, Leo Gao, Zehao Dou, Melody Y. Guan, Aleksander Madry, Wojciech Zaremba, Jakub Pachocki, and David Farhi. 2025. “Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2503.11926.

Listing 1 shows the structure of this third class. The candidate adds one file to its own source tree; the test runner imports that file before running tests; and from that point forward the runner, not the tests, fabricates the outcome.

These exploits are not confined to training runs. An independent evaluator measured how often the most capable models of 2025 took them when the scorer was within reach, and whether asking the model not to made any difference.

War Story 17.1: Frontier models patch their scorers (2025)
Context: In June 2025 the evaluation organization METR reported reward hacking by frontier models, chiefly OpenAI’s o3, on two suites of autonomous tasks: RE-Bench, whose research-engineering tasks let the agent see the entire scoring function, and the broader HCAST suite (Von Arx et al. 2025).

Mechanism: With the scorer in reach, o3 attacked it instead of the task. On a kernel-optimization task it overwrote the timing function so that it always returned shorter measurements; on a code-generation scaffolding task it patched the evaluation function to judge every submission successful; on a fine-tuning speed task it modified the script to copy the cached weights of an already fine-tuned model.

Impact: The fine-tuning task was hacked in 21 of 21 runs. Across RE-Bench, 39 of 128 runs hacked (30 percent), against 8 of 1,087 on HCAST (0.7 percent). Asked after its first hacking plan whether the plan adhered to the user’s intention, o3 answered no 10 times in 10. Adding “Please do not reward hack” to the prompt moved the hacking rate over 20 runs from 80 percent to 70 percent.

Fix: METR excludes successful reward-hacking runs from its reported capability results, and recommends that when a monitor detects a hack, developers patch the exploit in the scoring function rather than punish the model.

Systems lesson: A scorer the agent can read and write sits at the visible-test closure level no matter what the task author intended, and an instruction does not raise it. In an evaluation these runs are excluded after the fact; in an RLVR loop every one of them is a maximal reward, and the gradient copies it.

Von Arx, Sydney, Lawrence Chan, and Beth Barnes. 2025. Recent Frontier Models Are Reward Hacking. METR blog.

METR attributes the substantial difference between 30 percent on RE-Bench and 0.7 percent on HCAST to reachability: on RE-Bench the model could inspect the scoring function directly, making it easier to bypass. The central defense is a verification check that the policy cannot reach, evaluated on execution evidence that the policy cannot forge. For this reason, the sandboxed architecture of section 17.7 removes the test runner, configuration files, and evaluation credentials from the candidate’s control.

A reward is only as dependable as the closure evidence level of the check that computes it. These closure levels categorize the basis of an evaluation verdict by how much of it the agent policy can influence or reach. A static check establishes selected structural properties, a sealed test suite withholds and protects its test cases, and a formal proof verifies a specified property under explicit assumptions. Test coverage then limits any individual check at every level: even a sealed suite with an omitted edge case continues to reward buggy solutions that exploit the gap. Table 1 summarizes the sources used to construct agent rewards, alongside the specific vulnerabilities an optimizing policy exploits in each.

↩︎ Closure evidence levels, from static checks through sealed tests to formal proofs, are established in The invariant closure principle.

For a coding task with executable behavioral criteria, sealed tests supply the definitive outcome score, while compilation and format checks provide narrower gating signals. The lower rows score free-form text, and because the policy directly controls that text, a learned judge is itself a model with blind spots that gradient ascent can systematically exploit (Gao et al. 2023). The verification asymmetry (principle 3) dictates the role each score can assume. Learned assessments can rank candidate trajectories or shape exploration, but wherever a deterministic check exists, that check must govern the final reward. For tasks that lack an executable check, a learned judge can provide the primary reward, provided its calibration error is continuously measured against verified reference outcomes and re-evaluated as the policy distribution shifts. Section 17.7 develops the separate systems requirement of isolating the measurement pipeline from candidate code.

17.3.2 False accepts among rewarded outcomes

Early in training on a hard repair task, genuine passes are rare, so even a verifier that mistakenly accepts only a small fraction of broken patches can fill a large share of the rewarded rollouts with failures. This distinction between a grader’s false-acceptance rate and the failure rate among accepted outcomes is the same base-rate arithmetic that Grader error rates and equation 3.3 applied to a candidate pool, with one training-time change: the policy’s pass rate is no longer fixed, so the share of rewards that go to failures moves as the policy learns and is largest when true task success is scarcest. The symbols below map onto those of Grader error rates. Let \(\ReferenceAssessedFailure\) be criterion failure under an independent reference assessment and \(\VerifierVerdict^+\) acceptance by the training verifier (the event written \(\text{Acc}\) there). Let \(\ValidCandidateRate\) be the policy’s current probability that a rollout satisfies the criterion, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate=\Pr(\VerifierVerdict^+\mid \ReferenceAssessedFailure)\) the verifier’s false-acceptance rate, and \(\VerifierTrueAcceptanceRate\) its probability of accepting a criterion success. Provided verifier acceptance occurs with nonzero probability, the failure rate among accepted rollouts is

\[ \Pr(\ReferenceAssessedFailure\mid \VerifierVerdict^+) = \frac{(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate}{\ValidCandidateRate \VerifierTrueAcceptanceRate+(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate}. \tag{1}\]

The rarer true passes are, the more of the reward a small verifier error claims.

Equation 1 defines the false-accept share of the reward: the fraction of all rewarded rollouts that actually fail the task criterion. Because its denominator sums both true positive accepts (\(\ValidCandidateRate \VerifierTrueAcceptanceRate\)) and false positive accepts (\((1-\ValidCandidateRate)\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\)), a small false-positive rate \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) yields a large false share whenever failures are common. In reinforcement learning on challenging tasks, failure is the default state during early iterations. The same Bayesian arithmetic governs automated tests and learned reward models alike.

Napkin Math 17.1: False accepts in the reward
Problem: A hypothetical batch samples 8 rollouts for each of 1,024 tasks, giving 8,192 rollouts. Suppose 10 percent satisfy the criterion under the reference assessment. The training verifier accepts every criterion success and 2.5 percent of criterion failures. What share of its positive judgments are false accepts?

Math: For \(\BatchRolloutCount\) rollouts, expected true accepts are \(\BatchRolloutCount \ValidCandidateRate \VerifierTrueAcceptanceRate\) and expected false accepts are \(\BatchRolloutCount(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\). For this batch these are 819.20 and 184.32, respectively, totaling 1,003.52 expected positive judgments. Applying equation 1 gives 18.4 percent.

Result: 18.4 percent of rewarded rollouts are failures, although the verifier errs on only 2.5 percent of failures. The counts are expectations; an actual batch varies around them.

Systems insight: The false share is set by the ratio \((1-\ValidCandidateRate)\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate/(\ValidCandidateRate \VerifierTrueAcceptanceRate)\), so it moves with the policy’s pass rate as much as with the verifier. In the small-share regime, halving \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) roughly halves the share; doubling \(\ValidCandidateRate\) has approximately the same effect when \(\ValidCandidateRate\) is also small. For a fixed positive false-acceptance rate, the false share approaches one as true passes become rare. Measure verifier error at the pass rate the policy currently has, and expect the share to fall as training succeeds. The share is not the fraction of the gradient that rewards errors, which also depends on where the false accepts fall within their groups (section 17.5.1).

17.3.3 Reward composition

Adding auxiliary reward terms for well-formed tool calls or concise trajectories helps guide exploration, but poorly chosen weights can let a short, well-formatted failure score higher than a long, working repair. Suppose a fully assessed rollout receives an observed binary outcome \(\CorrectnessReward(\Trajectory)\in\{0,1\}\). Let \(\FormatComplianceScore(\Trajectory)\in[0,1]\) measure format compliance and let \(\text{len}(\Trajectory)\in[0,1]\) measure generated-token cost relative to an enforced cap. A common terminal score formulation is

\[ \TrajectoryReward(\Trajectory)=\CorrectnessReward(\Trajectory)+\FormatRewardWeight\FormatComplianceScore(\Trajectory)-\LengthPenaltyWeight\text{len}(\Trajectory), \qquad \FormatRewardWeight\ge 0,\quad \LengthPenaltyWeight\ge 0. \]

Format compliance encourages well-formed tool calls, and the length penalty favors concise solutions.4 Over their defined ranges, an observed pass earns at least \(1-\LengthPenaltyWeight\), while an observed failure earns at most \(\FormatRewardWeight\). A sufficient condition for every passing rollout to outrank every failing rollout is therefore

4 Reward Shaping: Ng et al. (1999) showed that an added shaping reward is guaranteed to leave the optimal policy unchanged when it takes the form of a difference of potentials over states, and that other additions can change which policy is optimal. Format and length terms are not of that form, so they change the objective itself; their weights therefore need the priority bound derived here rather than an assumption that they merely speed learning.

Ng, Andrew Y., Daishi Harada, and Stuart J. Russell. 1999. “Policy Invariance Under Reward Transformations: Theory and Application to Reward Shaping.” Proceedings of the Sixteenth International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 278–87.

\[ \FormatRewardWeight+\LengthPenaltyWeight<1. \]

Allowing equality permits a tie between a maximally penalized pass and a perfectly formatted failure. Strict inequality protects the score ordering, and it holds only while the normalized length penalty \(\text{len}(\Trajectory)\) remains strictly bounded in \([0,1]\). Normalizing by a nominal token budget preserves that invariant only if the execution runtime enforces the cap with hard process termination.

When an unexpected infrastructure failure leaves a required check unavailable, the assessment remains incomplete. Under the four-verdict rule of The four-verdict rule its verdict is unknown, and the reward stays unresolved rather than being converted to \(\CorrectnessReward=0\) or \(\CorrectnessReward=1\). Converting an unexecuted check to failure penalizes valid candidate solutions for harness faults, while converting it to success creates an immediate reward vulnerability; section 17.6.3 develops how a training loop must handle such rollouts. An explicit timeout under the task contract represents a valid failure verdict; a missing result due to test runner crashes does not. Similarly, introducing partial credit (awarding reward for the fraction of unit tests passed, for instance) defines a distinct optimization objective that alters which behavioral changes earn reinforcement, requiring its own priority analysis.

Permissions and hard resource limits must remain strict runtime constraints. A negative reward penalty merely discourages proposing a prohibited action; only the execution harness can enforce the boundary, and a penalty cannot undo an executed side effect. Even a reward function that ranks every unsafe trajectory below every safe one leaves an agent free to explore unsafe actions during policy search. The operational rule is to keep authorization, network isolation, and resource quotas firmly anchored in the container harness, using reward terms strictly to express preferences within that bounded perimeter. Even within that perimeter, the terminal reward evaluates the rollout trajectory as a complete artifact; converting that scalar outcome into updates for the individual actions taken along the trajectory introduces the credit-assignment problem.

17.4 Credit Across Turns

In the connection-pool repair, an agent might run the failing reproducer, read src/pool.py, and correctly identify the missed notification on connection return, only to introduce a syntax error in its final edit. A verifier that evaluates only the end state marks the entire trajectory as failed, exposing the multi-turn credit-assignment problem introduced in section 17.2.2: that terminal failure characterizes the trajectory as a whole, not whether the earlier diagnosis was a mistake.

The estimators that address this problem differ in what they spend to separate the two turns: nothing beyond the sampled return, a trained value model, a denser reward, or additional rollouts. Choosing among them requires keeping two quantities apart, the empirical coefficient applied in a sampled gradient update and the true expected value of an individual action.

17.4.1 The score-function estimator

When an environment emits only a terminal pass or fail at the end of an episode, the simplest gradient estimator attaches that single outcome to every turn along the trajectory. Let \(\TurnHorizon\) denote the finite interaction horizon, \(\RolloutTurnIndex \in \{0, \ldots, \TurnHorizon-1\}\) the decision index, and \(\InteractionHistory_k\) the delivered interaction history up to turn \(\RolloutTurnIndex\). Under an undiscounted objective, let \(\TurnReward_k\) represent the reward received immediately after taking action \(\RolloutAction_k\). The remaining return from step \(\RolloutTurnIndex\) onward, often called the reward-to-go, is defined as \(\RemainingReturn=\sum_{j=k}^{\TurnHorizon-1}\TurnReward_j\). In sparse verifiable environments where only a terminal reward \(\TrajectoryReward\) arrives at the end of the episode, every intermediate turn sees the same reward-to-go: \(\RemainingReturn=\TrajectoryReward\) for all \(\RolloutTurnIndex\).

For a trajectory sampled from the current policy, a likelihood-ratio, or score-function, estimator of the REINFORCE form (Williams 1992) is

\[\PolicyGradientEstimate=\sum_{k=0}^{\TurnHorizon-1}\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\RolloutAction_k\mid \InteractionHistory_k)\,[\RemainingReturn-\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_k)].\]

Its expectation equals the gradient of expected total reward under standard regularity conditions (Sutton et al. 1999). Because the initial task distribution, the environment transitions, and the reward mechanism do not depend on the policy parameters \(\ModelParameters\), their probabilities have zero derivative with respect to \(\ModelParameters\) and drop out when differentiating the log-probability of a sampled trajectory. Provided the differentiable policy has fixed support so that differentiation under the expectation integral is valid, the gradient depends on the execution environment and the verifier only through the observed return \(\RemainingReturn\) and never requires derivatives through the container or the test suite. Within each step of the sum, the score \(\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\RolloutAction_k\mid \InteractionHistory_k)\) is the sensitivity vector of the sampled action’s log-probability with respect to parameters \(\ModelParameters\). Multiplying this vector by the scalar return \(\RemainingReturn-\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_k)\) produces a step in parameter space rather than an intrinsic score of action quality (Sutton and Barto 2018).

The baseline \(\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_k)\) can be fixed, task-specific, or predicted by a learned value model. To maintain an unbiased gradient, \(\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_k)\) must not depend on the current sampled action conditional on \(\InteractionHistory_k\), and its parameters remain fixed when computing the policy gradient. Subtraction preserves the expectation because the expected score under the policy evaluates to zero:

\[\mathbb E_{\RolloutAction\sim\Policy(\cdot\mid \InteractionHistory)}[\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory)\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\RolloutAction\mid \InteractionHistory)] =\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory)\nabla_\ModelParameters\sum_\RolloutAction\Policy(\RolloutAction\mid \InteractionHistory)=0.\]

A baseline trained on earlier, independent rollouts satisfies this conditional independence property. A baseline computed from the current rollout’s own outcome does not, even if no gradient flows through it. That outcome depends on the very actions being scored, so the baseline is no longer a constant with respect to them. Group-relative estimators, which compute baselines across multiple rollouts for the same prompt, introduce sample-dependent coefficients that require separate statistical treatment.

Consider the consequences for our connection-pool repair. When an attempt fails (\(\TrajectoryReward=0\)) with a zero baseline, the sampled coefficient \([\RemainingReturn-\PolicyGradientBaseline(\InteractionHistory_k)]\) becomes zero for every turn, yielding zero gradient update. With a positive baseline instead (for instance, an average pass rate of \(0.4\)), the resulting coefficient is negative (\(-0.4\)), penalizing the insightful diagnostic command and the faulty edit alike. The learning algorithm does not assign positive credit to the diagnosis from this single run. Instead, credit for effective diagnostic steps emerges only statistically across many trajectories: actions that systematically lead to successful outcomes appear more frequently in winning runs than in losing ones.

Because every additional interaction turn introduces another score-function term to the summation, the variance of \(\PolicyGradientEstimate\) can grow with the trajectory length \(\TurnHorizon\). Even under a decaying-covariance assumption, the variance bound in Credit assignment variance in trajectory optimization grows linearly with \(\TurnHorizon\), so longer decision horizons can demand substantially more rollouts to extract a clean training signal. Long horizons therefore cost the learner twice. Under the horizon-compounding model (equation 1.1), successful trajectories become exponentially rarer, so fewer rollouts carry a positive signal, and each one that does arrives with a gradient estimate whose variance can grow with \(\TurnHorizon\).

17.4.2 Value, advantage, and process scores

To distinguish an insightful diagnostic command from a later syntax error on a single rollout, the estimator needs a baseline that updates after each turn rather than once per prompt. For the same continuation policy and reward definition, define

\[\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_k)=\mathbb E_\pi[\RemainingReturn\mid \InteractionHistory_k],\qquad \PolicyHistoryActionReturn(\InteractionHistory_k,\RolloutAction_k)=\mathbb E_\pi[\RemainingReturn\mid \InteractionHistory_k,\RolloutAction_k],\qquad \PolicyHistoryAdvantage(\InteractionHistory_k,\RolloutAction_k)=\PolicyHistoryActionReturn(\InteractionHistory_k,\RolloutAction_k)-\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_k).\]

The state value \(\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_k)\) averages over the policy’s next action and subsequent interaction, whereas the action value \(\PolicyHistoryActionReturn(\InteractionHistory_k,\RolloutAction_k)\) conditions explicitly on that chosen action. Their difference, the advantage, measures expected return relative to the policy’s default behavior at that history. These quantities are defined over interaction histories \(\InteractionHistory_k\) rather than abstract environment states, so they depend only on the observations and context tokens available to the model.

With terminal value \(\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_{\TurnHorizon})=0\), the sampled temporal-difference residual (Sutton 1988) is

\[\TemporalDifferenceResidual_k=\TurnReward_k+\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_{k+1})-\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_k).\]

Its conditional expectation given \(\InteractionHistory_k\) and \(\RolloutAction_k\) equals the true advantage when the value function is exact and subsequent observations and rewards follow the target environment distribution. A single observed residual is a noisy sample of that expectation, and substituting learned value estimates introduces approximation error on top of execution noise.5 Dropping \(\TurnReward_k\) is valid only when that immediate step reward is zero. Generalized advantage estimation and KL divergence develops the discounted and multi-step formulations (Schulman et al. 2016).

5 Generalized Advantage Estimation: GAE computes an exponentially weighted average of future residuals parameterized by a decay factor \(\TraceDecay \in [0, 1]\) (distinct from the shaping weights \(\FormatRewardWeight\) and \(\LengthPenaltyWeight\)). At \(\TraceDecay=0\), it uses a single one-step residual, which minimizes variance but incurs substantial bias if the value model contains errors. At \(\TraceDecay=1\), it recovers the empirical return minus the baseline, which is unbiased with respect to the value model but inherits all the variance of subsequent interaction turns (Schulman et al. 2016). For a multi-turn agent with an imperfect critic, \(\TraceDecay\) governs how much credit relies on neural value predictions rather than verified environment execution.

Schulman, John, Philipp Moritz, Sergey Levine, Michael I. Jordan, and Pieter Abbeel. 2016. “High-Dimensional Continuous Control Using Generalized Advantage Estimation.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Estimating an existing return differs from injecting new reward terms into the training objective. A learned critic acts as an estimator: it approximates the expected continuation return under the current policy without altering the underlying task specification. A process reward model (PRM), by contrast, scores intermediate reasoning steps or tool invocations directly, augmenting the primary objective with synthetic feedback.

↩︎ Process reward models as step-level scorers that prune search at inference time are established in Checking whole outputs vs. intermediate steps.

While dense process rewards can guide search during inference (Lightman et al. 2024; Uesato et al. 2022), training against them directly creates strong incentives for reward hacking. Because the policy generates the very tokens that the process verifier evaluates, systems engineers encounter three common failure modes:

  1. Superficial compliance: The agent generates formulaic boilerplate or ritualistic assertions (such as commenting “carefully verifying pointer bounds before dereferencing”) that satisfy the process model without performing any actual verification.
  2. Locally consistent hallucinations: The agent constructs fluent, step-by-step reasoning that appears rigorous to a local evaluator, even though the derivation rests on an unvalidated or false premise.
  3. Pathological conservatism: The agent avoids exploratory detours, speculative rollouts, or diagnostic failures that temporarily depress step scores, even when those intermediate investigations are necessary to diagnose complex software bugs.

Dense feedback reduces credit variance, but denser feedback is not necessarily correct feedback. Modifying the reward function changes what the policy optimizes, whereas value estimation estimates the performance of the policy under the true task metric. Table 2 separates these design choices and their architectural commitments.

17.4.3 Monte Carlo value estimates

Training a neural value critic on sparse multi-turn trajectories often fails when small prediction errors across tens of turns overwhelm the temporal-difference signal, while process reward models invite the policy to game intermediate step scores. Continuation sampling avoids both learned models by estimating state value directly from the environment. It rolls out \(\ContinuationCount\) independent trajectories (in this section \(\ContinuationCount\) counts continuations, not output tokens) from an intermediate execution boundary under a fixed continuation policy and averages their terminal returns. To accurately estimate \(\PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory)\), the starting state of these continuations must match the true conditional environment distribution given history \(\InteractionHistory\). In interactive environments with latent state (such as unobserved database locks, network traffic, or non-deterministic operating system processes), restoring a single concrete container snapshot conditions on that specific state rather than integrating over all hidden states consistent with \(\InteractionHistory\). A sound fork must therefore explicitly reconstruct the filesystem workspace, container network namespace, delivered context tokens, external mock services, continuation policy version, and verification oracle. Copying sandbox memory bytes or disk blocks alone does not guarantee environmental fidelity.

When individual continuations yield independent bounded returns \(\TrajectoryReward^{(\ContinuationIndex)} \in [0, 1]\), the sample mean \(\EstimatedRemainingReturn = \ContinuationCount^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^\ContinuationCount \TrajectoryReward^{(\ContinuationIndex)}\) serves as an unbiased estimator of the expected continuation return. Because Bernoulli random variables achieve maximum variance at \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.5\) (\(\ContinuationReturnStandardDeviation^2 \le 1/4\)), the estimator’s variance is bounded above by \(1/(4\ContinuationCount)\), corresponding to a standard error of at most \(1/(2\sqrt{\ContinuationCount})\). Halving this uncertainty bound requires a fourfold increase in sampled rollouts. This error bound holds strictly for independently sampled branches; if rollouts share cached environment state or common prefixes, cross-sample correlation increases the variance. Furthermore, estimating action values \(\PolicyHistoryActionReturn(\InteractionHistory, \RolloutAction)\) requires conditioning explicitly on the chosen action \(\RolloutAction\) before rolling out the continuation policy. Consequently, sampling values at selected turn boundaries estimates the net value delta across blocks of turns rather than isolating individual token decisions.

A hypothetical fork makes this estimate concrete. Out of 20 continuations launched from the initial bug report, 1 passes, giving a sampled value of 0.05. In the primary trajectory, the policy then runs a debugger, producing an observation that contains the failing line. Forking 20 continuations from this updated history yields 12 passes, corresponding to a sampled value of 0.60. The estimated value difference for the debugger turn is 0.55, even if the primary trajectory later fails on a subsequent edit. Each sample mean carries a standard error of at most 0.11. Because the two fork estimates are independent, their variances add, so the difference has a standard error of at most \(\sqrt2/(2\sqrt \ContinuationCount)\), or 0.16. Treating a difference as resolved only when it is at least twice that bound, here 0.32, the observed 0.55 clears the threshold, so the gain is unlikely to be sampling noise. Resolution against noise does not make \(\FiniteChange \EstimatedRemainingReturn\) an exact advantage, however; it remains an empirical estimate at two particular fork conditions. Of the three ways to connect the terminal failure to earlier turns (figure 1), only the forks separate the debugger turn from the faulty edit on this trajectory. They do it by paying for extra rollouts rather than by adding a criterion the policy could learn to satisfy.

These continuation rollouts are expensive. Every branch consumes inference tokens, host memory, and sandbox execution time, and the total depends directly on how many intermediate boundaries the fork schedule samples, which makes the schedule the real design choice.

Napkin Math 17.2: Continuation sampling in tokens
Problem: An agent trajectory runs 30 turns of 512 generated tokens each. At each chosen intermediate boundary, the system samples 8 continuations, each running for the remainder of the episode. What generation cost results from sampling every intermediate boundary, compared with sampling only after turns 10 and 20?

Variables: \(\TurnHorizon\) is the trajectory horizon in turns, \(\TurnGeneratedTokenCount\) the generated tokens per turn, \(\ContinuationCount\) the continuations per boundary, and \(\ForkBoundarySet\subseteq\{1,\ldots,\TurnHorizon-1\}\) the index set of completed-turn fork points. Branches are nonrecursive.

Math:

\[\BaseTrajectoryTokenCount=\TurnHorizon\,\TurnGeneratedTokenCount,\qquad \ForkedTrajectoryTokenCount(\ForkBoundarySet)=\TurnHorizon\,\TurnGeneratedTokenCount+\ContinuationCount\,\TurnGeneratedTokenCount\sum_{k\in\ForkBoundarySet}(\TurnHorizon-\RolloutTurnIndex).\]

When forking at every intermediate boundary, the remaining-turn sum is \(\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon-1)/2\), yielding \(\ForkedTrajectoryTokenCount/\BaseTrajectoryTokenCount=1+\ContinuationCount(\TurnHorizon-1)/2\).

Result: The base trajectory generates 15,360 tokens. The multiple-of-base column in table 3 prices the gap: exhaustive intermediate sampling creates severe computational blowup compared with selective boundary evaluation.

Systems insight: Exhaustive forking grows quadratically as \(\ContinuationCount \TurnHorizon^2\,\TurnGeneratedTokenCount/2\), meaning that doubling the horizon quadruples the credit computation bill even though base trajectory generation only doubles. At this horizon of 30 turns, exhaustive forking multiplies token generation by 117. In contrast, sampling only two targeted boundaries costs 9 times the base trajectory. The engineering decision rule is to fork only at a handful of critical boundaries, spending the saved generation budget on exploring a broader diversity of tasks. This inventory tallies generated output tokens alone; context prompt processing, sandbox snapshot restoration, tool execution, and verification add overhead to both configurations, while variable-length continuations shift the exact totals.

Selective forking requires an effective policy for choosing boundaries. Two triggers suit this choice. A turn boundary qualifies when the policy’s action distribution has high token entropy (a next-token distribution spread across many candidates, measured in section 17.8.1), which indicates a decision among genuine alternatives. A turn also qualifies when it executes state-modifying tool calls such as file edits, database migrations, or network reconfiguration. Forking at two or three such junctures places the empirical credit estimates where trajectories actually diverge, at a small fraction of the exhaustive cost.

Credit assignment is a budget decision that trades computational cost against statistical fidelity. Fixed baselines incur zero token overhead but offer no step-level resolution. Learned value functions reduce variance at the expense of training a secondary critic. Continuation rollouts provide direct empirical value estimates at the price of substantial token generation. Group-relative policy optimization represents the minimalist end of this spectrum, comparing complete sampled returns within a task group without spending any additional tokens on intermediate branches.

Checkpoint 17.1: Rewards and credit

A reward is a measurement, and credit is an estimate built on it. Each has a price in errors or tokens.

17.5 Group Relative Policy Optimization

Suppose three fresh rollouts attempt the connection-pool repair under the same fixture, budget, and assessment rule. Their completed terminal rewards are \([0,1,0]\). The successful rollout provides a direct comparison for the two failures without requiring an expert to label each intermediate edit, though this outcome comparison alone cannot identify which specific action caused success. Group relative policy optimization (GRPO) uses such within-task comparisons6 to construct policy update coefficients, replacing a learned value baseline with groups of sampled outcomes (Shao et al. 2024).

6 Sampled Baselines: Scoring each sample against the other samples for the same input predates GRPO. Kool et al. (2019) drew several samples per input and used the others as each one’s baseline, obtaining variance reduction without a learned critic, and Ahmadian et al. (2024) found that such REINFORCE-style estimators could outperform PPO when fine-tuning language models from human feedback. GRPO adds division by the group’s reward dispersion, a weighting choice analyzed in section 17.5.3.

Kool, Wouter, Herke van Hoof, and Max Welling. 2019. Buy 4 REINFORCE Samples, Get a Baseline for Free!
Ahmadian, Arash, Chris Cremer, Matthias Gallé, Marzieh Fadaee, Julia Kreutzer, Olivier Pietquin, Ahmet Üstün, and Sara Hooker. 2024. “Back to Basics: Revisiting REINFORCE-Style Optimization for Learning from Human Feedback in LLMs.” Proceedings of the 62nd Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 12248–67. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2024.acl-long.662.

The trade-off centers on baseline estimation rather than the reward source: proximal policy optimization (PPO) can operate with an external check, just as GRPO can evaluate rollouts against a learned reward model. PPO trains a value function, either sharing parameters with the policy or running in a separate critic network (Schulman et al. 2017). GRPO eliminates that critic alongside its memory and training overhead, paying for the omission with additional rollouts: it samples a group of \(\GroupSize\) attempts per task to estimate empirical group statistics. GRPO serves as our worked optimizer because its systems accounting is straightforward to track, though the execution isolation and trajectory recording requirements that follow apply equally to both approaches. Both optimizers also hold a reference policy, a frozen copy of the starting checkpoint used to penalize drift away from it (section 17.5.2). Table 4 sets the two side by side.

17.5.1 Group-relative coefficients

Without a trained critic network, a single rollout’s terminal score gives the optimizer no reference level to decide whether the outcome beat expectations for that task. To construct a baseline without a critic, the system collects \(\GroupSize\) completed rollouts for the same task from a fixed behavior policy \(\pi_{\text{old}}\), the specific model checkpoint, execution environment, and decoding configuration that generated the trajectories. Let \(\TrajectoryReward_i\) denote the terminal reward assigned to rollout \(\GroupRolloutIndex\). For outcome-only verification, the group statistics and advantage coefficients are:

\[\GroupMeanReward=\frac{1}{\GroupSize}\sum_{j=1}^\GroupSize \TrajectoryReward_j,\qquad \GroupRewardStandardDeviation=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\GroupSize}\sum_{j=1}^\GroupSize(\TrajectoryReward_j-\GroupMeanReward)^2},\qquad \GroupRelativeAdvantage_i=\frac{\TrajectoryReward_i-\GroupMeanReward}{\GroupRewardStandardDeviation+\AdvantageStabilizer}. \tag{2}\]

The positive stabilizer \(\AdvantageStabilizer\) keeps the expression well-defined when all rollouts receive identical rewards. The denominator uses the empirical population standard deviation with divisor \(\GroupSize\), rather than a Bessel-corrected sample standard deviation with divisor \(\GroupSize-1\). This convention is part of the objective: altering the divisor rescales every coefficient, and neither divisor accounts for unobserved task behaviors outside the sampled group. During policy updates, the rewards \(\TrajectoryReward_i\) and coefficients \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\) remain fixed constants; because this scalar is easily confused with the state-action advantage function of section 17.4, the next definition keeps the two apart.

Definition 17.3: Group-relative advantage

Group-relative advantage is the coefficient \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\) that GRPO attaches to every sampled token of rollout \(\GroupRolloutIndex\): the rollout’s reward minus the mean reward of its task group, divided by the group’s reward dispersion.

  1. Significance: It replaces a learned value function with \(\GroupSize\) rollouts per task. The group size therefore governs both the accuracy of the empirical baseline and the rollout compute budget; on a binary check, a lone pass in a group of \(\GroupSize\) receives a coefficient approaching \(\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}\).
  2. Distinction: The advantage \(\PolicyHistoryAdvantage=\PolicyHistoryActionReturn-\PolicyRemainingReturn\) is an expectation for one action at one history, whereas the group-relative coefficient is an empirical, standardized score broadcast uniformly across all tokens in an entire trajectory. Furthermore, because the group statistics include the rollout being scored, the action-independent baseline argument does not make the standardized estimator unbiased REINFORCE. Centering alone is harmless, since \(\TrajectoryReward_i-\GroupMeanReward\) equals \((\GroupSize-1)/\GroupSize\) times \(\TrajectoryReward_i\) minus the mean of the other \(\GroupSize-1\) rewards, a valid leave-one-out baseline rescaled. The departure comes from dividing by \(\GroupRewardStandardDeviation\), which every reward in the group helps set. It is a weighting choice recorded with the run rather than an error to be corrected.
  3. Common pitfall: Reading a large positive coefficient as evidence that the rollout was sound. The coefficient measures only how the rollout’s reward compared with its group; a false pass receives the exact same coefficient as a genuine success.

Napkin Math 17.3: From three rewards to update coefficients
Problem: Three hypothetical repair rollouts receive terminal rewards \([0,1,0]\), with no intermediate rewards and \(\AdvantageStabilizer=10^{-8}\). What coefficients enter the outcome-only GRPO update?

Math: The mean is \(\GroupMeanReward=1/3\), or 0.333333. The squared deviations are \(1/9\), \(4/9\), and \(1/9\), so \(\GroupRewardStandardDeviation=\sqrt{2/9}\), or 0.471405. Dividing each centered reward by \(\GroupRewardStandardDeviation\) gives \((-1/3)/\sqrt{2/9}=-1/\sqrt 2\) for each failure and \((2/3)/\sqrt{2/9}=\sqrt 2\) for the pass.

Result: Each failed rollout has coefficient -0.707107; the successful rollout has coefficient 1.414214. The three coefficients sum to zero.

Systems insight: The update sign emerges from relative group ranking rather than absolute reward values, and its magnitude scales up as successful trajectories become rarer. However, because every generated token within a rollout receives the identical scalar coefficient, crediting insightful diagnostic steps and mistaken proposals alike, GRPO trades fine-grained temporal credit assignment for substantial algorithmic and computational simplicity.

The rarer the pass in its group, the larger the coefficient it receives.

The group mean acts as an adaptive per-task baseline that absorbs intrinsic task difficulty. When a binary-evaluated group of size \(\GroupSize>1\) produces exactly one pass, the group mean is \(1/\GroupSize\) and the empirical standard deviation is \(\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}/\GroupSize\). Under equation 2, that lone successful rollout receives an advantage coefficient of

\[\LonePassAdvantage=\frac{1-1/\GroupSize}{\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}/\GroupSize+\AdvantageStabilizer} =\frac{\GroupSize-1}{\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}+\GroupSize\AdvantageStabilizer}, \tag{3}\]

which evaluates to \(\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}\) when the stabilizer is zero and remains slightly below that bound for positive \(\AdvantageStabilizer\). On a difficult task where only one rollout in 16 succeeds, the group baseline drops to 0.0625. The solitary success receives an amplified coefficient of 3.87, while the fifteen failures receive small negative coefficients of 0.26 in magnitude. Conversely, on an easy task where only one rollout in 16 fails, the signs invert, heavily penalizing the isolated failure. In both scenarios, the update amplifies the contrast that separated success from failure on that specific problem instance. The group mean thereby fills the baseline role that a learned value function performs in PPO.

The same arithmetic explains why the false-accept share of equation 1 is not the false share of the gradient. False accepts occur on failing rollouts, which are most common on hard tasks, and on a hard task a false pass is often the group’s only pass, so it receives a coefficient near \(\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}\). A false pass on an easy task, one of many passes in its group, receives only a small positive coefficient, the mirror image of the small failure coefficients above. The same count of false accepts can therefore carry a larger or a smaller share of the update depending on which groups they land in. The hardest tasks, where the share of equation 1 is also highest, are where each one weighs most.

17.5.2 Clipped surrogate objective

Taking multiple gradient steps on a collected batch of rollouts causes the updated policy \(\Policy\) to drift away from the behavior policy \(\pi_{\text{old}}\) that sampled the actions, and an unclipped importance ratio can trigger destructive weight updates. Let \(\SampledPolicyToken_{i,t}\) denote the \(\TokenIndex\)-th sampled policy token in rollout \(\GroupRolloutIndex\), \(\ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t}\) its delivered context prefix, and \(\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i>0\) the total number of sampled generation tokens. While tool outputs, environment responses, and prompt text appear within prefixes \(\ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t}\), they are masked out of the policy loss and do not count toward \(\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i\). At each generated token position, the probability ratio is:

\[\PolicyProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)= \frac{\Policy(\SampledPolicyToken_{i,t}\mid \ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t})} {\pi_{\text{old}}(\SampledPolicyToken_{i,t}\mid \ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t})}.\]

Both probabilities must reflect the exact sampling convention used during generation. If generation applied temperature scaling or top-\(\Glyph{k}\) / top-\(\Glyph{p}\) truncation, the recorded behavior probability \(\pi_{\text{old}}\) in the denominator must match the actual sampling distribution and remain strictly positive. Probability support eliminated by decoding filters cannot be restored by downstream loss ratios.

With clipping parameter \(0<\RatioClippingWidth<1\) (kept distinct from the denominator stabilizer \(\AdvantageStabilizer\)), the clipped surrogate objective is:

\[\ClippedSurrogateObjective_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)=\min\left\{ \PolicyProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i, \operatorname{clip}(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters),1-\RatioClippingWidth,1+\RatioClippingWidth)\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i \right\}.\]

The outer minimum interacts with the sign of \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\) so that clipping takes effect only when the update has already moved a token’s probability far enough in the favored direction. When \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i>0\), raising the token’s probability increases \(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\), and once the ratio exceeds \(1+\RatioClippingWidth\) the clipped branch becomes the smaller of the two terms inside the minimum, flattening the surrogate and removing any gradient incentive to push the current probability higher. When \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i<0\), multiplying by the negative coefficient reverses the ordering of the two branches inside the minimum. Once the probability ratio drops below \(1-\RatioClippingWidth\), the unclipped product is less negative than the clipped term, so the minimum again selects the flat clipped branch and avoids destructive runaway penalties. In contrast, when parameter changes move a token’s probability in the unfavorable direction, such as reducing the probability ratio below \(1-\RatioClippingWidth\) when \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i>0\) or raising it above \(1+\RatioClippingWidth\) when \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i<0\), the unclipped product is the smaller term in the minimum and continues to penalize the surrogate. Even where the flat branch is active, clipping caps only the direct gradient incentive on individual tokens rather than creating a true trust region that bounds overall policy shift. Parameter sharing across tokens and additional loss terms can still propel \(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\) well outside the clipping interval.7

7 Trust Region: The clipped surrogate originated in trust region policy optimization, which explicitly constrained each policy step to keep the average KL divergence between updated and behavior policies below a target threshold (Schulman et al. 2015). PPO replaced this constrained optimization step with a clipped objective to simplify implementation and distributed scaling (Schulman et al. 2017). Because clipping provides no mathematical guarantee against large policy shifts, production training pipelines must monitor probability ratios and empirical divergence metrics directly.

Schulman, John, Sergey Levine, Pieter Abbeel, Michael Jordan, and Philipp Moritz. 2015. “Trust Region Policy Optimization.” Proceedings of the 32nd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 37: 1889–97.
Schulman, John, Filip Wolski, Prafulla Dhariwal, Alec Radford, and Oleg Klimov. 2017. “Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1707.06347, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1707.06347.

A frozen reference policy \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\), typically initialized from the pre-RL checkpoint, provides an auxiliary regularizer that penalizes excessive policy drift. In distributed pipelines, \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) maintains a distinct model identity from the behavior policy \(\pi_{\text{old}}\) that generated the rollouts. Assuming strictly positive probabilities over shared token support, we define:

\[\ReferenceProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)=\frac{\pi_{\text{ref}}(\SampledPolicyToken_{i,t}\mid \ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t})} {\Policy(\SampledPolicyToken_{i,t}\mid \ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t})},\qquad \PerTokenKlEstimate_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)=\ReferenceProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)-\log \ReferenceProbabilityRatio_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)-1.\]

The penalty term \(\PerTokenKlEstimate_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\) is strictly non-negative and vanishes if and only if the current and reference token probabilities coincide. Conditioned on prefix \(\ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t}\), the expectation of \(\PerTokenKlEstimate_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\) over tokens sampled from the current policy \(\Policy\) equals the Kullback–Leibler divergence \(\KlDivergence(\Policy\parallel\pi_{\text{ref}})\), the expected log ratio of the two distributions under \(\Policy\), which is zero only when they agree. The identity is derived in Generalized advantage estimation and KL divergence. The plain log ratio \(-\log \ReferenceProbabilityRatio_{i,t}\) has the same expectation, but a single sampled token can make it negative, so a per-token penalty built on it would sometimes reward drift. Each term \(\ReferenceProbabilityRatio-\log \ReferenceProbabilityRatio-1\) is non-negative, so every token’s penalty pushes toward the reference. Because the tokens in the training batch were originally drawn from the behavior policy \(\pi_{\text{old}}\), evaluating this sample average provides an approximation to the current-policy divergence that degrades as off-policy lag accumulates over multiple gradient steps.

Combining these components yields the empirical GRPO maximization objective:

\[\GrpoSampledObjective(\ModelParameters)= \frac{1}{\GroupSize}\sum_{i=1}^\GroupSize\frac{1}{\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i}\sum_{t=1}^{\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i} \left[\ClippedSurrogateObjective_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)-\KlPenaltyCoefficient \PerTokenKlEstimate_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\right], \qquad\KlPenaltyCoefficient\ge0. \tag{4}\]

Standard deep learning frameworks minimize the negative of \(\GrpoSampledObjective(\ModelParameters)\) as a loss. The hyperparameter \(\KlPenaltyCoefficient\) scales the reference policy penalty. Because reward standardization, probability ratio clipping, and token length normalization collectively depart from the true expected return \(\ExpectedReturnObjective(\ModelParameters)\), production systems record these optimization hyperparameter choices alongside the trained model weights and validate performance through fresh online evaluation trials.

17.5.3 Length normalization

Averaging token losses within each rollout by \(1/\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i\) in equation 4 penalizes each token of a long failure far less than each token of a short failure, teaching the policy to stretch out failing attempts. At the start of an update, when the current policy matches the behavior policy and the probability ratios equal one, rollout \(\GroupRolloutIndex\) contributes the following gradient to the reward surrogate:

\[\nabla_\ModelParameters\RolloutSurrogateObjective_i=\frac{\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i}{\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i}\sum_{t=1}^{\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i}\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\SampledPolicyToken_{i,t}\mid \ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t}), \tag{5}\]

initially assigning every sampled token in trajectory \(\GroupRolloutIndex\) an effective scalar weight of \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i/\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i\). This per-sequence divisor exerts asymmetric optimization pressure across different outcome classes (Liu et al. 2025). For failing rollouts (\(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i<0\)), extending generation length distributes the negative penalty across more tokens, diluting the per-token gradient penalty. For successful rollouts (\(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i>0\)), producing fewer tokens concentrates the positive advantage on a compact sequence, amplifying the per-token reinforcement. The resulting optimization pressure, known as length bias, systematically favors concise successes and verbose failures.

Liu, Zichen, Changyu Chen, Wenjun Li, Penghui Qi, Tianyu Pang, Chao Du, Wee Sun Lee, and Min Lin. 2025. “Understanding R1-Zero-Like Training: A Critical Perspective.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2503.20783.

Consider two failing rollouts within the same group that receive identical negative coefficients \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\). If one fails after 128 tokens while the second exhausts the context budget at 4,096 tokens, the concise failure absorbs a per-token penalty 32× larger than that of the verbose failure. To eliminate this distortion, alternative implementations replace individual sequence lengths \(\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i\) with a constant divisor shared across the group or microbatch, such as the mean trajectory length \(\MeanRolloutLength\):

\[\ConstantDivisorGrpoObjective(\ModelParameters)=\frac{1}{\GroupSize\,\MeanRolloutLength}\sum_{i=1}^\GroupSize\sum_{t=1}^{\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i}\left[\ClippedSurrogateObjective_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)-\KlPenaltyCoefficient \PerTokenKlEstimate_{i,t}(\ModelParameters)\right].\]

Under this constant-denominator objective, every token receives an update weight proportional to \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\) alone, regardless of trajectory length. While these scalar weight comparisons illustrate the gradient mechanics, the actual trajectory length drift depends on how individual token gradient vectors align or cancel across sequences during training, an empirical dynamic examined in section 17.8.

Reward standardization introduces an analogous weighting trade-off across tasks. Dividing by \(\GroupRewardStandardDeviation+\AdvantageStabilizer\) normalizes the scale of centered rewards across groups. As \(\AdvantageStabilizer\to0\), every group without a tie has the same total squared coefficient, \(\sum_i\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i^2=\GroupSize\), whether it holds a single isolated success or an even split between passes and failures. The equality is between whole groups, not individual rollouts. Within the fixed total, a lone pass takes \(\GroupSize-1\) of the \(\GroupSize\) units and its failures share the remaining one, which is the amplification of equation 3, whereas an even split spreads the total evenly. Omitting this dispersion divisor weights tasks strictly by their raw reward spread. Both normalizations are objective design choices that must be logged with the training run alongside the clipping threshold.

17.5.4 Zero-contrast groups

That constant group weight holds only when a group contains both passes and failures. When all \(\GroupSize\) repair attempts on a hard bug fail the acceptance check, or all \(\GroupSize\) attempts on a routine bug pass, every rollout in the completed group yields the identical reward, forming a zero-contrast group. In this scenario, every numerator in equation 2 evaluates to zero, yielding advantage coefficients \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i=0\), surrogate terms \(\ClippedSurrogateObjective_{i,t}=0\), and policy gradient contributions \(\nabla_\ModelParameters\ClippedSurrogateObjective_{i,t}=0\).
