The reward-driven component of the policy gradient vanishes entirely, leaving only the reference policy penalty and any auxiliary regularizers active. Identical rewards do not imply identical behavior, because different reasoning paths or tool failures collapse into the same uninformative binary score.

Assuming independent rollout attempts on a task with fixed success probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\), a group of size \(\GroupSize\) contains at least one success and at least one failure with probability \(\GroupContrastProbability(\GroupSize,\AttemptPassProbability)=1-\AttemptPassProbability^\GroupSize-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\GroupSize\). This matches the informative-task fraction defined in equation 15.3 with \(\IndependentAttemptCount=\GroupSize\), where the excluded outcomes represent the all-pass and all-fail edge cases. (This contrast probability differs from pass@\(\GroupSize\), which requires only a single pass.)

Computational waste is greatest in the difficult task regimes where the learning signal is needed most. Sampling groups of 2 on a task with pass rate \(\AttemptPassProbability=\) 0.10 yields a contrast probability \(\GroupContrastProbability\) of only 0.18, leaving 82 percent of generated groups without any policy gradient signal. Even with larger groups of 8, a task solved on 5 percent of attempts produces zero reward gradient 66 percent of the time. Every uninformative group incurs the full systems cost of model inference, containerized tool execution, and reward verification. This binomial model assumes independent trials on a homogeneous task; evaluating a heterogeneous workload requires computing expected contrast per difficulty bucket, as aggregate fleet averages obscure the true zero-contrast rate.

Groups of eight carry a gradient reliably only at mid-range pass rates.

Because binary outcome variance \(\AttemptPassProbability(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\) peaks at \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.5\), active task scheduling based on rolling pass rates maximizes the proportion of informative rollouts. Systems engineers can counter zero-contrast waste using two levers: increasing group size \(\GroupSize\) or filtering task queues by difficulty. The two levers carry different systems costs. Comparing them mirrors the cost-per-accepted-task framework introduced in equation 1.2: the total computational investment per task attempt divided by the probability of producing usable gradient information.

Napkin Math 17.4: Rollouts per informative group
Problem: A hypothetical update samples 8 rollouts for each of 512 tasks, with each rollout executing 30 interaction turns generating 512 tokens per turn. Under the baseline task distribution, the agent policy succeeds on 5 percent of attempts. What is the total generation cost per training step in tokens, and which scaling strategy purchases informative gradient signal more efficiently: doubling group size to 16, or filtering the dispatch queue for tasks where the agent succeeds roughly 50 percent of the time?

Math: Generating a single trajectory consumes 30 \(\times\) 512 = 15,360 tokens, bringing the total generation burden across 4,096 rollouts to 62.9 million tokens per step. With binary outcome probability \(\GroupContrastProbability=1-\AttemptPassProbability^\GroupSize-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\GroupSize\), the amortized rollout cost per informative group is \(\GroupSize/\GroupContrastProbability\), as detailed in table 5.

Result: Curriculum difficulty targeting reduces rollout cost per informative gradient by 2.9× compared to the hard baseline mix, whereas doubling group size actually increases the rollout cost per usable gradient. The metric counts informative groups, not the information inside each one. A group of 16 estimates its baseline more precisely than a group of 8, so the verdict holds where the binding problem is obtaining any contrast at all. Targeting also shifts the task mixture, which the run must log (section 17.9.1).

Systems insight: The metric \(\GroupSize/\GroupContrastProbability\) quantifies the computational cost per informative gradient signal: the numerator represents rollout expenditure per group, while the denominator measures the probability that the group yields usable contrast. In regimes with low baseline pass rates, \(\GroupContrastProbability\) dominates system efficiency, and it responds far more dramatically to curated curriculum selection than to expanding group size. When obtaining any contrast is the binding constraint and the changed task mixture is acceptable, the demonstrated curriculum purchases informative groups more cheaply than the larger group. Baseline precision, required task coverage, and measured held-out learning progress can justify other group sizes; the run must record the task-selection distribution as well as the chosen group size.

While group-relative comparisons establish advantage coefficients and optimization weights, every term in the objective ultimately resolves to conditional token probabilities. These probabilities remain valid only if the training engine faithfully reconstructs the precise token contexts seen by the model during inference. In multi-turn agentic workflows, model generations interleave with tool executions, environment state mutations, and dynamic context compaction. Executing GRPO correctly in production therefore requires an exact, immutable log of the tokens passed to and sampled from each model invocation, rather than an unversioned end-of-episode transcript.

17.6 Multi-Turn Agentic RL

In a single-turn response, the policy generates one contiguous block of tokens from one static prompt, so the final text holds the entire training example. In a multi-turn repair trial, however, the agent alternates between proposing actions (code edits, shell commands, or structured tool invocations) and reading observations returned by compilers, test runners, and the container runtime, while the harness compacts or truncates older turns to fit the context window.

That interactive loop breaks single-turn training assumptions in three places: earlier conditioning prefixes are overwritten as the context window compacts, environment observations sit interleaved with sampled policy tokens, and infrastructure timeouts or node preemptions can cut an episode off mid-flight. Preserving a valid gradient update across those turns, whether under GRPO or any other multi-turn policy-gradient optimizer, requires capturing each model invocation before compaction alters its prefix, masking non-policy tokens out of the loss denominator, and separating task-budget failures from censored runs.

17.6.1 Invocation records

Consider a concrete execution: trial rl_trial_01 running against pool_fixture_v1. In turn 1, the model invokes a test suite. The harness runs the suite and delivers a truncated failure log. In turn 2, the model conditions on that log and proposes a targeted source patch. By turn 4, context window pressure forces the harness to compress the earlier diagnostic logs into a concise two-line summary. If the training pipeline subsequently attempts to reconstruct the rollout from the final episode transcript, it confronts a corrupted causal graph: turn 2 would appear conditioned on the two-line summary rather than the full failure log that actually prompted the patch. Action probabilities computed against that retrofitted context score the policy’s decisions against information states the agent never encountered during sampling.

To guarantee replay fidelity, the rollout recorder must capture each invocation’s delivered input token prefix alongside the exact token IDs emitted by the generator, linking both to a durable interaction record. Storing full token arrays for every step across millions of rollouts would quickly exhaust cluster storage, so systems deduplicate shared prefixes with immutable content-addressed blobs or prefix-tree references. Along with those prefix references, the record registers the tokenizer version, chat template, attention masks, rotary position offsets, and compaction decisions that shaped the prompt. Storing raw UTF-8 text and re-tokenizing it during training cannot substitute for the recorded token IDs. Small differences in BPE merge rules, whitespace handling, or tool-schema formatting shift token boundaries and sequence lengths, so only the captured IDs preserve the trajectory that was actually sampled. Table 6 extends the input-fidelity principles established in Serializing Trajectories to the sampling evidence required by multi-turn reinforcement learning.

17.6.2 Policy actions and conditioning context

Even with every invocation’s input prefix and output tokens preserved in table 6, only a fraction of the tokens in a multi-turn rollout were chosen by the policy; the rest were supplied by the user prompt, the tool harness, or the container environment. To connect those recorded invocations to the mathematical objectives developed earlier, we map the episode into the flattened policy-token index \(\TokenIndex=1,\ldots,\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i\) from section 17.5.2 for rollout \(\GroupRolloutIndex\), while retaining the structural mapping to invocation \(\RolloutTurnIndex\) and the local generation offset. Each conditioning prefix \(\ConditioningTokenPrefix_{i,t}\) consists of the exact input context delivered to invocation \(\RolloutTurnIndex\) concatenated with the preceding policy tokens generated within that invocation.

When computing action-scoring losses, the distinction between conditioning and generation is absolute. Prompts, environment feedback, tool payloads, and harness scaffolding act exclusively as conditioning context. They propagate activations through the model to shape downstream attention and predictions, but they are not sampled policy actions; their token log probabilities must never appear in the loss objective. Consequently, loss normalization denominators must count only the scored policy tokens, while the runtime cost accounting tallies prefill processing and decode generation as separate operational line items.

This action-scoring mask serves a different purpose from the imitation masks used in supervised fine-tuning. In the supervised recovery example, the malformed proposal e2 and its refusal stay in the prefix as context but carry a zero loss mask, so they are never imitation targets, because the objective is imitation of curated behavior (Observation Loss Masking). In reinforcement learning, however, the mask identifies the support of the exploration distribution, and verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) admits every sampled action, weighted by its verified outcome. If the policy samples an invalid JSON schema or an unparsable shell command during exploration, that sequence is an authentic policy action, even if the execution harness subsequently refuses to dispatch it. Masking those tokens out because a curator would not present them as demonstrations corrupts the estimator. The malformed tokens escape the negative update, and the failing rollout’s whole negative coefficient lands on its remaining well-formed tokens, so the update pushes down valid syntax in place of the mistake. Similarly, when the model generates an end-of-turn delimiter or stop token, that token is an action that consumed probability mass. By contrast, an abrupt terminator injected by a dataloader when cutting off an execution is harness intervention, not policy choice, and must be excluded. Any partial-action scoring strategy requires an explicit mathematical objective and denominator rather than an ad hoc mask inherited from a supervised training pipeline.

The denominator of the importance-sampling probability ratio must represent the true behavior distribution that generated the token. Inference engines often apply sampling transforms that renormalize the distribution over a restricted subset of the vocabulary, such as temperature scaling, top-\(\Glyph{k}\) truncation, nucleus (top-\(\Glyph{p}\)) filtering, or grammar-constrained finite-state decoding. The rollout record must therefore store the normalized chosen-token log probability under the active generation configuration, along with the sampling parameters required to reconstruct the sampling state. When computing policy updates, the trainer evaluates the numerator under the target policy \(\Policy\) using the identical prefix recorded during generation. Substituting unconstrained model logits into the denominator treats the behavior policy as an unconstrained softmax. Some systems adopt this simplification to avoid caching rollout probabilities, but it introduces estimator bias that must be tracked explicitly as an approximation.

Freezing the policy checkpoint across each episode substantially simplifies this accounting. In high-throughput asynchronous architectures where weight updates occur concurrently with rollout generation, an agent might execute invocation 1 on checkpoint \(\PolicyCheckpoint_1\) and invocation 2 on checkpoint \(\PolicyCheckpoint_2\). If an infrastructure design permits cross-invocation updates within a single trial, the record must associate behavior checkpoints and generation probabilities per invocation. Tagging the entire rollout with a single model version misattributes the provenance of later tokens.

Once token log probabilities and behavior distributions are properly recorded, the system links the trajectory-level advantage coefficient \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\) to each sampled policy token. If the system assigns advantages at the turn level rather than across the entire trajectory, it must adhere to the estimator and continuation rules detailed in section 17.4. Two parallel rollouts that happen to reach turn 3 may possess radically different interaction histories, execution latencies, and tool states; comparing intermediate outcomes purely by matching turn indices provides an invalid foundation for relative reward scoring.

17.6.3 Stopping and censored rollouts

Attaching a terminal reward to those scored tokens assumes the episode reached a valid stopping point, yet a repair rollout that halts at 5 minutes because it exhausted its 10-turn task budget and one that halts at 3 minutes because the cluster scheduler reclaimed its host node look identical if the harness logs only that execution stopped. How the learning algorithm interprets an enforced stop depends entirely on the task specification and what triggered the halt. If a task contract defines success as accepted code completion within a declared budget of 10 turns and 5 minutes, exhausting that budget without reaching a solution constitutes an authentic task failure. The policy legitimately receives a zero or negative reward. The rollout record must log the exact termination reason and resource metrics, noting whether the boundary interrupted active model generation or an external tool execution.

Other interruptions stem from external operational limits rather than policy choices. For example, a single tool invocation exceeding a 30 s sandbox deadline produces an error observation from which the agent can still recover. Alternatively, an assessment microservice may crash, leaving the verifier unable to score an otherwise complete pull request. If an orchestration worker aborts an active rollout because the cluster scheduler reclaims the node or the batch queue deadline expires before task budget exhaustion, the collector produces a censored rollout. The rollout record must treat budget exhaustion, observation recovery, verifier unavailability, and infrastructure preemption as mutually exclusive execution states. They map onto the four-verdict rule of The four-verdict rule. Budget exhaustion is a fail. A recovered tool error is an ordinary observation inside the trajectory. Verifier unavailability and preemption leave the outcome pending while a retry is still possible and unknown once it is not. Throughout this chapter, a censored or unresolved rollout means one in the last two states, whose reward is missing because of the infrastructure rather than the policy.

Mishandling censored rollouts biases the training data in one of two opposite directions. Silently discarding incomplete rollouts introduces classic survivorship bias, purging complex, multi-turn trajectories from the training set and skewing policy optimization toward trivial, fast tasks. Conversely, assigning a default reward of zero to rollouts interrupted by infrastructure outages penalizes the agent for distributed system failures, teaching the policy to avoid long multi-step trajectories. The training pipeline must declare an explicit policy for censored episodes: waiting for asynchronous verifier recovery, scheduling idempotently bounded retries, or applying statistical imputation methods that account for missing outcomes without biasing advantage estimates.

Finally, terminating policy sampling does not automatically stop pending asynchronous operations. Dispatched bash subshells, spawned containers, and cloud API calls remain active until explicitly cancelled. The harness must track resource ownership across the full operational lifecycle, ensuring that abandoned or censored rollouts clean up orphaned processes and container volumes regardless of whether the resulting trajectory is admitted into an optimization batch.

17.6.4 Collection and deployment contracts

Those turn caps, context compaction policies, observation truncation limits, and tool refusal responses are not passive safety rails; they define the Markov decision process that the policy explores. If the training environment diverges from the intended deployment contract, the agent learns behavioral adaptations that fail in production: a policy trained with a generous 50-turn allowance can learn meandering trial-and-error debugging strategies that collapse when placed under a strict 10-turn production limit. When teams intentionally expand training budgets as an exploratory curriculum to bootstrap discovery in sparse-reward environments, the transfer penalty under tighter deployment constraints must be tracked as an explicit empirical metric.

Decoupling model invocation from episode lifetime is equally important for cluster efficiency. Tool-heavy agent rollouts can spend most of their lifetime waiting for external work: a policy might generate a tool request in 200 ms, followed by a 45 s wait while an external container compiles code and runs a test suite. An accelerator held for that rollout during the wait would compute for under 1 percent of the episode, and the loss compounds across every rollout in the training cluster. High-throughput collection engines decouple inference serving from episode execution, scheduling active invocations from other ready rollouts onto the accelerator while blocked rollouts wait for external tool observations. This interleaving maximizes hardware utilization without changing the causal observation stream consumed by any individual trajectory. Section 17.9 develops the bounded queues, group completion barriers, and version-tracking protocols required to coordinate that asynchronous schedule at scale. A rollout record can give a faithful account of what the policy observed, what tokens it generated, and how the environment responded. That recorded trajectory can still guide policy updates safely only if an uncompromised boundary separates the artifacts the policy is permitted to modify from the independent harness that evaluates them.

17.7 Verifier Isolation

Consider the representative trial rl_trial_01, in which an agent submits a candidate patch after running local unit tests against an edited connection pool. The reinforcement learning loop requires an authoritative verdict under the evaluation specification repair_accept_v1, not an unverified self-report from the environment that generated the patch. As the METR evaluations in section 17.3 illustrate, an agent capable of modifying its evaluation environment can bypass the intended task and rewrite its own scoring logic, and where the scorer is within reach it often does.

To prevent this failure mode, the learning loop puts the grader isolation of Grader isolation on the reward path, with held-out tests kept by a trusted controller outside the candidate’s sandbox. We call this architectural guarantee verifier isolation: the assessment harness and its verdict are protected from both the executing agent policy and the candidate code it submits.8 Whereas test coverage determines whether a test suite exercises the required behavioral contract, verifier isolation guarantees that the emitted verdict reflects the actual execution of those tests rather than unauthorized tampering.

8 Complete Mediation of the Verdict: The tool boundary of Complete mediation checks every access to every protected object; verifier isolation applies the same rule, with least privilege, to the reward path. The policy never needs write access to test definitions or score records, so the runtime grants none, and every route to a verdict, including module resolution paths and test runner binaries, passes through the authoritative controller.

17.7.1 Separate assessment workspaces

Grading a patch inside the same container where the agent explored leaves the test runner, module imports, and exit codes exposed to the candidate’s modifications. To close that path, the controller separates the exploration workspace from the assessment workspace (table 7). When an episode finishes, the controller captures the candidate artifact, binds it to the rollout identifier and base source revision, and admits it only if its diff modifies files the task permits, such as application source rather than tests, grading scripts, or container configuration. The controller then applies that admitted diff to a fresh copy of the declared fixture in a separate assessment workspace. Because the test suite must import or execute the patched application to evaluate it, the candidate remains untrusted even after it crosses into the assessment container. Application code that runs inside the same language interpreter process as the grader can still monkeypatch assertion objects or reporting hooks in memory, and hidden test files mounted read-only inside the candidate’s filesystem namespace can still be opened and read by the application at runtime. The assessment harness must therefore keep the authoritative verdict store and protected test definitions outside the process and filesystem scope reachable by the code under test, treating candidate stdout, stderr, and exit codes strictly as telemetry rather than verdicts.

Reinforcement learning adds a failure mode that offline evaluation seldom meets, the exploitation of exception handling in the verification harness. A candidate patch that hangs, triggers an unhandled memory fault, or crashes the test runner can convert an inevitable test failure into a missing verdict. If the training pipeline silently drops rollouts with missing verdicts from batch normalization, or assigns them a default score higher than a failure, the policy learns to generate crash-inducing code rather than viable solutions. The resource caps declared for the candidate’s own execution decide whether a crash or timeout counts against the candidate. A breach of those limits, such as an infinite loop that exhausts a 60 s execution budget, is candidate-induced. Any such resource exhaustion or process crash maps to a failure (\(\TrajectoryReward = 0\)) and never outscores an orderly test failure. A failure of the host or grader while the candidate stayed within its limits, such as an out-of-memory crash caused by host oversubscription, is infrastructure and leaves the outcome unresolved under the rules of section 17.6.3. In neither case may the system emit a default pass, and training telemetry must report the two outcomes separately.

Isolating the workspace does not stop information from leaving through permitted outputs, the confinement problem that agent sandboxes face on their egress channels (Name resolution and local destinations). Even binary pass/fail responses leak information across successive submissions. In multi-turn rollouts, intermediate feedback must never expose hidden test names, assertion details, or internal stack traces, which would let the policy reconstruct the hidden suite turn by turn. The training loop must therefore declare what feedback reaches the agent and keep it to the minimum the task needs. Tests queried repeatedly to generate training rewards also become de facto training data, so an unbiased release assessment requires evaluation suites that the training loop never executes.

17.7.2 Repeated checks and noisy rewards

Verifier isolation eliminates deliberate tampering, but it cannot prevent non-deterministic evaluation outcomes. Flaky checks, caused by thread scheduling races, network jitter, or asynchronous event loops, can spuriously accept an incorrect patch or fail a valid one, even when the test definitions are completely protected. Repeating the check reduces the rate of accidental acceptance. If a test suite is under-specified, however, an incorrect patch that satisfies those partial requirements will pass every repetition.

Let \(\TrialPassProbability\) represent the probability that a fixed candidate patch passes a single verification trial under a specified protocol. Assuming \(\VerificationTrialCount\) independent trials share the same pass probability, the all-pass rule, which requires unanimous success across all \(\VerificationTrialCount\) trials, accepts the candidate with probability

\[\Pr(\text{accept candidate})=\TrialPassProbability^{\VerificationTrialCount}.\]

In this notation, \(\VerificationTrialCount\) denotes the total number of trials conducted, including the first. An initial pass followed by \(\VerificationTrialCount\) additional confirmation reruns would instead yield an acceptance probability of \(\TrialPassProbability^{\VerificationTrialCount+1}\). When trials are statistically dependent, the all-pass probability becomes the product of conditional probabilities \(\prod_{j=1}^{\VerificationTrialCount} \Pr(\text{pass}_j\mid\text{all earlier passes})\), which can diverge sharply from \(\TrialPassProbability^{\VerificationTrialCount}\). Launching fresh container instances with randomized execution seeds promotes independence, whereas a persistent checker bug or shared host-level bottleneck introduces positive correlation across runs.

Group normalization makes a flaky pass as damaging as a tampered one. On a hard task, a lone false pass receives the same near-\(\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}\) coefficient (equation 3) as an authentic solution, so the optimizer spends its largest updates on scheduling luck. The all-pass trade-off between accidental-reward risk, false rejections, and extra verification work is priced in notebook 17.5.

Napkin Math 17.5: The cost of an all-pass rule
Problem: How does requiring repeated passes change accidental reward, false rejection, and verification work?

Variables: In a hypothetical fixture, a fixed incorrect patch has trial pass probability \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\) of 0.20, while a fixed correct patch has trial pass probability \(\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\) of 0.98. Trials are independent under an unchanged protocol. Acceptance requires \(\VerificationTrialCount\) total passes, with \(\VerificationTrialCount\) set to 3; verification stops at the first rejection. A separate illustration considers a group of 16 independently assessed incorrect candidates with the same \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\).

Math: A single trial for each incorrect candidate gives group probability \(1-(1-\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability)^\GroupSize\) of at least one false pass, or 97.2 percent. In the special case of exactly one pass, its standardized coefficient approaches \(\sqrt{\GroupSize-1}\), approximately 3.87, as \(\AdvantageStabilizer\) approaches zero. The all-pass rule lowers each incorrect candidate’s acceptance probability to \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability^{\VerificationTrialCount}\), or 0.8 percent, but rejects the correct candidate with probability \(1-\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability^{\VerificationTrialCount}\), or 5.88 percent.

Trial \(\VerificationTrialIndex+1\) is needed only if the preceding \(\VerificationTrialIndex\) trials passed. Consequently,

\[\mathbb E[\ExecutedTrialCount\mid \TrialPassProbability]=\sum_{j=0}^{\VerificationTrialCount-1}\TrialPassProbability^\VerificationTrialIndex.\]

For the incorrect patch, \(1+\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability+\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability^2\) gives 1.24 trials; for the correct patch, \(1+\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability+\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability^2\) gives 2.9404. Running every trial regardless of earlier outcomes instead consumes \(\VerificationTrialCount\) trials per candidate.

Systems insight: Each added trial multiplies an incorrect patch’s acceptance by \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\) and a correct patch’s by \(\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\), so the rule pays when \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\) is far below \(\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability\). Here 3 trials cut an incorrect patch’s acceptance from a per-trial 0.20 to 0.8 percent, at a false-rejection cost of 5.88 percent for the correct one. Stopping at the first rejection makes the cost fall mostly on correct candidates, which run nearly all \(\VerificationTrialCount\) trials, so size the verifier pool for the passing fraction of the population, not for one trial per candidate.

A real candidate population mixes pass probabilities. Because the expected trial workload \(\sum_{j=0}^{\VerificationTrialCount-1} \TrialPassProbability^\VerificationTrialIndex\) is convex in \(\TrialPassProbability\), substituting the population’s mean pass probability directly into the formula underestimates total compute demand by Jensen’s inequality. High-quality candidates progress deeper into repeated trials than low-quality ones, concentrating the verification workload on the most promising rollouts. Sizing verification capacity, measured in completed candidate evaluations per second, therefore requires weighting the trial demand by the empirical distribution of candidate quality rather than using a single average pass rate.

When successive runs disagree, repetition alone is insufficient, because the divergence indicates an unstable test harness that requires its own repair. Verification harnesses should deliberately vary test execution order and process scheduling, running regular sanity checks against known-valid and known-invalid reference artifacts to detect fixture drift before emitting training rewards. If a specific test case stays flaky across runs, operators must quarantine it from the active reward loop. Because quarantining alters the task’s reward function, all test suite revisions must be versioned, preserving the historical trial records and audit trail. Verifier isolation and repeated assessment address the named tampering and flakiness paths; policy quality remains bounded by the properties the protected checks cover and requires independent release evidence.

Checkpoint 17.2: Optimizing agent trajectories

The recorded interaction, group statistic, and protected assessment must describe the same training event.

17.8 Training Pathologies

Even when an execution sandbox isolates the grader and every gradient step updates cleanly, reinforcement learning can steer an agentic policy into unhelpful or degenerate behaviors. Training curves alone rarely reveal why: for a complex repair task, rising reward accompanied by longer rollouts might indicate thorough automated testing, repetitive failed edits, or merely a shift in which tasks completed grading before timing out. Systematically diagnosing these pathologies requires connecting shifts in sampled token distributions, rollout lengths, reward sub-components, and gradient statistics to concrete hypotheses, because each failure mode demands a distinct systems intervention.

17.8.1 Diversity and token entropy

When a policy commits prematurely to a single failing strategy on unsolved tasks, its sampled rollouts repeat the same tool calls and stop exploring the alternatives needed to discover a passing repair. Token entropy tracks this narrowing at the token level by measuring uncertainty in a specified next-token distribution. For a delivered prefix \(\TokenPrefix\) and vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary\),

\[\NextTokenEntropy\bigl(\Policy(\cdot\mid \TokenPrefix)\bigr) =-\sum_{\VocabularyToken\in\TokenVocabulary}\Policy(\VocabularyToken\mid \TokenPrefix) \log\Policy(\VocabularyToken\mid \TokenPrefix),\]

with natural logarithms giving entropy in nats and \(0\log0\) interpreted as zero. When logging this metric, we must explicitly distinguish the raw base-model distribution from the post-sampling distribution shaped by temperature scaling, top-\(\Glyph{k}\) filtering, or grammar constraints. Adjusting inference parameters alters measured entropy without changing network weights, just as changes in benchmark difficulty or prompt length skew aggregate averages, so any reported entropy number is incomplete without specifying the prefix population and decoding pipeline that generated it. Even with those held fixed, low entropy is appropriate at deterministic syntax positions, such as emitting closing brackets or fixed tool schemas. Rather than setting an arbitrary global entropy threshold, engineers should inspect action divergence and tool-call variety on unsolved fixtures alongside token-level uncertainty.

Uniform rewards within a sample group introduce a similar diagnostic ambiguity. When all \(\GroupSize\) trajectories in a group receive identical scores, the zero-contrast update of section 17.5.4 zeroes out the reward-surrogate gradient. This zero-contrast condition is an algebraic property of group normalization, but its root cause varies: diverse attempts might all fail, diverse valid solutions might all pass, or an uncalibrated test harness might fail to distinguish partial progress. Disentangling these mechanisms requires inspecting the actual trajectory logs rather than relying on summary statistics. Table 8 maps common training observations to their underlying ambiguities and practical diagnostic checks. To ensure that an apparent change in policy behavior is not merely an artifact of shifting measurement conditions, all diagnostic comparisons must hold the task fixtures, sampling parameters, reward functions, and grader versions strictly constant.

17.8.2 Output length

An unchanged average reward often conceals an expensive failure migration, where cheap syntax failures early in training turn into prolonged, compute-intensive execution loops that still fail verification. Conversely, a verbose trajectory may reflect thorough exploratory testing that culminates in a working patch, whereas a terse rollout may indicate early abandonment. Because generated token counts, tool invocation counts, and wall-clock execution time capture distinct execution costs across those cases, tracking mean trajectory length alone is rarely sufficient; engineers should inspect full length distributions segmented by outcome (accepted, failed, pending, and unknown).

The length bias of section 17.5.3 is the first mechanism to audit when failing trajectories lengthen unexpectedly, because per-rollout averaging charges each token of a long failure less than each token of a short one. Training the same fixed diagnostic tasks under the per-rollout and the constant-denominator objectives separates growth caused by that incentive from growth that reflects useful work, such as additional diagnostic checks that the environment reward favors.

When system requirements favor concise solutions, any length penalty introduced into the reward function must respect the preference hierarchy established in section 17.3. Under bounded reward structures, enforcing \(\FormatRewardWeight+\LengthPenaltyWeight<1\) guarantees that any passing trajectory scores higher than any failing trajectory, regardless of length. A poorly calibrated length penalty that causes a verbose successful repair to score lower than an immediate failure corrupts task incentives. Reward-based length penalties influence policy preferences only during learning; hard execution budgets (such as maximum token limits or turn timeouts) must remain strictly enforced by the execution runtime.

17.8.3 Entropy collapse and regularization

Standard symmetric ratio clipping provides a direct algorithmic route to entropy collapse, the premature loss of action diversity on tasks the agent has not yet solved. When an agent discovers a sequence that partially succeeds early in training, the positive advantage boosts the probabilities of those tokens, rapidly sharpening the distribution around that single path. The positive-advantage surrogate in section 17.5.2 stops increasing at a policy ratio of \(1+\RatioClippingWidth\), but this incentive threshold acts with extreme asymmetry in absolute probability space.

With \(\RatioClippingWidth\) set to 0.2, a dominant token already at probability 0.9 has an incentive threshold of 1.08. Because this threshold exceeds 1.0, the surrogate continues rewarding increases throughout the remaining probability mass. In contrast, for an alternative candidate token at probability 0.01, the positive surrogate incentive flattens after an absolute probability increase of 0.002. Gradients from other tokens and loss terms can still move a token’s probability ratio beyond the clipping interval. Rarely sampled actions therefore face a steep climb to regain probability, while dominant actions consolidate their lead. As action diversity drops across unsolved tasks, the \(\GroupSize\) rollouts in each group collapse into near-identical trajectories. Their rewards tie, precipitating the zero-contrast state described in section 17.5.4 where the reward-surrogate gradient vanishes.

To counteract this dynamic, the clip-higher formulation decouples the clipping boundaries into an asymmetric interval \([1-\LowerRatioClippingWidth, 1+\UpperRatioClippingWidth]\) with \(\UpperRatioClippingWidth > \LowerRatioClippingWidth\) (Yu et al. 2025). This wider upper bound allows low-probability tokens backed by positive advantages to climb more rapidly, while the lower bound still curbs policy drift for penalized tokens. As always, diagnostic context matters: low entropy at deterministic syntax positions is normal and desirable; the operational danger is collapsing diversity among tool invocations and decision branches on unsolved tasks.

Yu, Qiying, Zheng Zhang, Ruofei Zhu, Yufeng Yuan, Xiaochen Zuo, Yu Yue, Weinan Dai, et al. 2025. “DAPO: An Open-Source LLM Reinforcement Learning System at Scale.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2503.14476.

Alternative regularizers attack this problem through different mechanisms. An explicit entropy bonus directly penalizes sharp token distributions across targeted positions. A reference-policy divergence penalty, such as the \(\KlPenaltyCoefficient\)-weighted term of equation 4, instead penalizes drift from the initial base model, though it enforces no minimum entropy floor of its own. Both approaches modify the loss surface and require controlled experimentation: hold task distributions and sampling parameters fixed, evaluate on downstream task success and token efficiency, and track policy-ratio distributions. A smoother training loss is weak evidence of healthy exploration; only verified task completion counts.

When exploration collapses, an agent can enter repetitive loops (emitting duplicate shell commands or re-reading unchanged files) where runtime watchdog mechanisms must intervene. However, repetitive behavior in multi-turn environments is inherently ambiguous: waiting for a slow build, polling an asynchronous API, or retrying a failed test after adjusting parameters can mimic an infinite loop. Systems should log the exact termination trigger and apply the stop-state rules of section 17.6.3, which score budget exhaustion as a task failure and keep infrastructure interruptions out of the reward. Terminating a runaway rollout caps resource consumption immediately, but it settles nothing outside the slot; environment state and external side effects still pass to their cleanup handlers.

↩︎ Runtime watchdog mechanisms for detecting and terminating repetitive agent loops are established in Semantic Watchdogs.

17.8.4 Recovery behavior

Reports such as the 2025 DeepSeek-R1 study document spontaneous increases in response length during pure reinforcement learning (R1-Zero), accompanied by natural-language tokens where the model appears to reconsider prior reasoning steps (DeepSeek-AI et al. 2025). From a systems perspective, however, introspective tokens such as “Wait, let me double-check” are merely generated text, not verified cognitive state. In an agentic system interacting with real infrastructure, the sole criterion for genuine recovery behavior is whether subsequent tool invocations successfully correct an execution failure or adapt to unexpected environmental feedback.

DeepSeek-AI, Daya Guo, Dejian Yang, et al. 2025. “DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2501.12948, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2501.12948.

Consider testing recovery on the connection-pool repair task. A diagnostic evaluation fixture can intentionally inject an environmental disturbance, for example a dependency version conflict introduced immediately after the agent completes its initial inspection. The evaluation then measures whether the policy gathers fresh diagnostic data, revises its patch to satisfy the conflicting constraints, and successfully passes the test suite within the original resource allowance. An evaluation must track failed and unhandled executions alongside successful runs, so that apparent recoveries do not reflect hidden timeout artifacts. Designing experiments around controlled perturbations provides empirical evidence of recovery under the tested perturbation, just as targeted recovery demonstrations mitigate compounding errors during imitation learning.

↩︎ Recovery trajectories as a remedy for compounding errors in imitation learning are established in Exposure Bias.

Matched-budget evaluations, in which candidate policies and baselines operate under identical token and turn limits, reliably separate productive deliberation from empty verbosity. Generating this diagnostic evidence at scale demands robust infrastructure: a versioned catalog of task fixtures, structured rollout traces with explicit execution states, and sufficient isolated grader capacity. These diagnostic demands directly link algorithmic health to the systems architecture of the rollout pipeline.

17.9 Environments and Rollouts

Before a single repair rollout can contribute to a parameter update, it passes through pipeline stages whose latencies span two orders of magnitude: a model inference step samples tokens in tens of milliseconds, stateful tool commands and test suites inside pool_fixture_v1 run for seconds, and cold sandbox provisioning can take longer still. Coordinating model inference, isolated sandboxes, automated assessment suites, and trace storage across those disparate timescales is the central systems challenge of verifiable reinforcement learning. The pipeline must keep every stage saturated while preserving the statistical contract of the learning algorithm, maximizing rollout throughput without silently altering, truncating, or biasing the distribution of trajectories delivered to the optimizer.

17.9.1 Task fixtures and reset conditions

A recorded trajectory from a previous failure cannot be replayed as a reinforcement learning task on its own. Without a reproducible container image, pinned dependencies, and a verified grading suite, candidate rollouts fail on missing packages or residual files left by earlier runs. Every rollout therefore begins with a concrete task environment governed by a fixture contract that defines the initial filesystem state, available tool APIs, network permissions, and reward evaluation criteria.

↩︎ Collection fixtures, which layer a collection contract on the evaluation gym’s execution baseline, are established in Task Fixture Design.

Data splitting requires the same discipline before any task is sampled or filtered into a curriculum, keeping related problem variants, mutations, and solution-equivalent tasks on the same side of the split. Training on familiar repositories teaches local code conventions, whereas holding out entire repository families tests whether the policy transfers to unfamiliar codebases. Within each split, ground-truth reference solutions and known defective candidates calibrate the verifier so that flaky dependencies and harness faults show up as operational errors rather than false rejections. Because filtering tasks by difficulty to sharpen reward contrast also shifts the task mixture, the pipeline must log the active curriculum rather than reading a higher training reward as a gain across the uncurated population.

↩︎ Task groups, which keep every variant of a problem on one side of the split, are established in Evaluation claims and task groups.

Between successive episodes, an environment slot executes a rollout reset to restore the fixture to its declared initial state. Its obligations are those of the trial reset in State reset between trials, which terminates lingering processes, scrubs writes and credentials, and settles outstanding operations before the slot is reused. Snapshots restore local disk state in milliseconds but leave external side effects to teardown handlers. Controlled stochasticity, such as randomized problem seeds or initial state permutations, must be codified directly in the fixture specification. While offline benchmarks incur reset overhead only intermittently, reinforcement learning loops execute resets across millions of rollout cycles, making reset efficiency a direct driver of aggregate throughput.

17.9.2 Environment-cycle time and throughput

A cold container reset takes several seconds, whereas a snapshot restore finishes in milliseconds, so the reset mechanism can easily take as long as the model inference and tool calls of the episode itself. To price how much capacity a faster reset actually recovers, consider an environment slot executing agent trajectories serially. Let \(\TurnHorizon\) be the horizon (the number of interaction turns), \(\ModelTime\) the model inference time per turn, \(\ToolTime\) the tool execution time per turn, \(\RolloutAssessmentTime\) the final assessment duration, and \(\EnvironmentResetTime\) the environment reset time. Assuming constant durations, no intra-slot concurrency, and zero queueing delay, the total slot cycle time is

\[\EnvironmentCycleTime=\TurnHorizon(\ModelTime+\ToolTime)+\RolloutAssessmentTime+\EnvironmentResetTime. \tag{6}\]

Accelerating the reset routine directly shortens this cycle, but by a bounded amount. Amdahl’s law sets that bound. Speeding up one stage of a fixed workload shortens the whole by at most the time that stage occupied, so the overall speedup is limited by that stage’s share of the total. Multiplying the reciprocal of \(\EnvironmentCycleTime\) by the total number of environment slots gives the theoretical capacity ceiling of the rollout fleet. However, that projection assumes model inference servers, tool executors, verification sandboxes, and storage pipelines can sustain this aggregate rate without contention. We compute this baseline capacity in notebook 17.6 before examining where downstream shared bottlenecks arise.

Napkin Math 17.6: Reset cost and slot capacity
Problem: A hypothetical pool contains 256 environment slots. Each episode has 20 decisions, each using 120 ms of model service and 80 ms of tool execution, followed by 0.5 s of assessment. Cold reset takes 3.5 s; snapshot reset takes 15 ms. What capacity change follows under the serial-slot assumptions?

Math: Under the serial-cycle model in equation 6, non-reset work takes 4.5 s per cycle. Cold reset gives a cycle of 8 s, of which 56.25 percent is non-reset work. Snapshot reset gives a cycle of 4.515 s and a non-reset fraction of 99.67 percent. For either case, idealized pool capacity is the number of slots divided by cycle time.

Result: The corresponding capacities are approximately 115,200 and 204,120 episodes per hour, a 1.77× local capacity ratio. The non-reset fraction includes tool and assessment time, rather than representing accelerator utilization alone.

Systems insight: Reducing cold reset from 3.5 s to the stated snapshot latency yields a 1.77× slot-capacity gain; eliminating reset entirely would leave the 4.5 s of active work as the cycle-time floor. This is Amdahl’s law applied to the slot. The capacity ratio is \(\ColdResetCycleTime / \SnapshotResetCycleTime\), and because the active work term remains fixed, optimizing reset latency yields diminishing returns once reset overhead is a small fraction of the cycle. Furthermore, these recovered slot-hours translate into training updates only if downstream inference serving, verification workers, storage pipelines, and the training loop possess sufficient headroom to process 1.77× as many episodes. In a balanced pipeline, the stage with the least headroom dictates the realized throughput gain.

↪ Amdahl’s law bounds the speedup that a fixed serial fraction allows, as developed in The coordination tax.

17.9.3 Complete-group scheduling

The serial-slot model prices each environment slot in isolation, but group-relative training dispatches \(\GroupSize\) rollouts for the same task as a cohort and consumes their completed trajectories in minibatches. At dispatch, that shared task prompt is an advantage: because all \(\GroupSize\) rollouts start from an identical prompt token sequence under matching model weights and compatible cache allocation policies, a radix-tree prefix cache computes the prompt’s key-value states once across the cohort (principle 5), whereas promoting a new policy checkpoint invalidates those cached entries and forces a cold start even on identical prompts. Once the \(\GroupSize\) rollouts begin executing, however, each one interleaves bursty model inference with blocking tool waits while training workers wait for completed groups. Allocating distinct resource pools for inference and training decouples those workloads, whereas colocated or alternating execution schedules reduce model weight broadcast and network synchronization costs depending on the relative runtimes of rollout generation and gradient computation.

↩︎ The weight, exact-prefix, and residency conditions for prefix-cache reuse are established in Prefix compatibility.

When the training loop synchronizes across rollout groups before updating parameters, the trainer enforces a group barrier. The step duration is dictated by the slowest member of the cohort:

\[\PolicyUpdateStepTime=\max_i \TrajectoryDuration(\Trajectory_i)+\TrainerUpdateTime, \tag{7}\]

Every worker slot that finishes earlier sits idle until the tail completes. Because variable tool execution times, multi-turn reasoning loops, and compiler or test runs give agent trajectories heavy latency tails, that tail wait can leave a large share of the pool idle.

For a hypothetical group of 4 whose members complete between 0.2 s and 16.8 s, equation 7 holds all four slots until the 16.8 s member finishes, stranding 65 percent of reserved slot capacity in an idle state. This barrier penalty, charted in panel (a) of figure 2, motivates releasing slots early and gathering rollouts asynchronously.

Under group-relative objectives, asynchronous rollout completion does not remove the mathematical requirement for an aggregated group baseline. The scheduler assigns the group identifier, fixture configuration, planned cohort membership, and generating checkpoint version prior to dispatch. Each completed member record and its verification status buffer in memory until the group closure policy determines eligibility. If the objective requires verified rewards across all group members, group closure must hold the record until all members report. If an environment terminates due to turn caps or time limits, the orchestrator applies the declared protocol penalty. Infrastructure failures such as sandbox crashes or network timeouts stay unresolved under the rules of section 17.6.3, and the closure policy must say whether the group waits for them or closes without them.

As soon as a rollout finishes, its environment slot frees its inference concurrency and sandbox resources for subsequent work, retaining only its compact trajectory record in an aggregation buffer while evicting key-value cache states from accelerator memory. Because rollouts complete out of order, group buffers, verification queues, and training ingestion queues require bounded capacities in both record count and total bytes. Admission controllers must track in-flight slot allocations alongside buffered records, applying backpressure upstream to prevent intermediate queues from overflowing host memory during bursty completions.

Prematurely closing a group with only its fastest-finishing members distorts the baseline sample. Slower trajectories often represent harder subtasks, alternative multi-step tool interactions, or transient infrastructure contention. Recomputing relative advantages across only the early completions fails to recover the true underlying distribution; early truncation imposes a survival bias that favors fast, simplistic solutions. Training pipelines must explicitly log all exclusions and their causal categories, monitor completion latency distributions, and track task coverage shifts over time. Similarly, filtering out zero-variance groups (where all members receive identical rewards) reduces gradient variance in advantage-based policy surrogates, but it also eliminates training signals intended for auxiliary objectives, such as reference-policy divergence constraints.

17.9.4 Policy staleness

Decoupling rollout collection from training updates means trajectories can arrive after the optimizer has already advanced the policy weights. The version disparity between the policy that sampled an action and the policy undergoing optimization is policy staleness. Asynchronous actor-learner frameworks such as IMPALA address this discrepancy by pairing asynchronous scheduling with off-policy corrections,9 reweighting trajectory tokens by the probability ratio between the learner and actor checkpoints (Espeholt et al. 2018). For an episode pinned to a single behavior checkpoint, let \(\BehaviorPolicyVersion\) denote its generating version and \(\text{ver}_t\) the trainer’s active version. When the training loop steps at a steady pace, the update-count lag is

9 V-trace: IMPALA’s correction weights each step by the ratio of learner to actor probabilities, truncated at a ceiling, accepting some bias to keep the variance of long products of ratios in check (Espeholt et al. 2018). Truncation matters more for agent rollouts than for short episodes, because a product of per-token ratios over thousands of tokens is otherwise dominated by a few extreme values.

Espeholt, Lasse, Hubert Soyer, R’emi Munos, Karen Simonyan, Vlad Mnih, Tom Ward, Yotam Doron, et al. 2018. “IMPALA: Scalable Distributed Deep-RL with Importance Weighted Actor-Learner Architectures.” Proceedings of the 35th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 1407–16.

\[\FiniteChange\text{ver}=\text{ver}_t-\BehaviorPolicyVersion\approx\left\lfloor\frac{\RolloutAdmissionAge}{\PolicyUpdateStepTime}\right\rfloor, \tag{8}\]

where \(\RolloutAdmissionAge\) is the wall-clock time elapsed from sampling the first prompt token to admission into the training batch, and \(\PolicyUpdateStepTime\) is the training step latency. This relationship enables system designers to calibrate staleness bounds directly from rollout duration. Long agent rollouts are precisely the trajectories most prone to severe staleness. Bounded staleness has a long lineage in distributed training architectures, where worker parameter reads are constrained to a fixed step horizon (Ho et al. 2013), and asynchronous actor-learner algorithms tolerate bounded lag in exchange for high actor utilization (Mnih et al. 2016). Applied at admission (figure 2, panels b and c), the check admits the two short rollouts and excludes the long one, recording why it was dropped. These are record-level lag decisions, not a complete group-admission rule. Under an objective that requires the planned cohort, every member must also have a resolved reward and satisfy that bound before the group enters an update.

While the version counter provides an operational threshold for admission control, distribution divergence requires separate monitoring: a single large optimizer step can shift output probabilities far more dramatically than several incremental updates. Production diagnostics must break down elapsed age into generation time, tool wait states, verification latency, group buffer residence, and ingestion queue delay, while tracking empirical probability-ratio distributions (\(\Policy / \pi_{\text{old}}\)) to detect distribution drift directly. Logging per-invocation metadata as described in section 17.6 accurately captures that behavior distribution even when policy checkpoints update mid-episode. Training on such composite trajectories requires optimization formulations that support turn-by-turn behavior probabilities, because retroactively relabeling an entire trajectory under the newest policy checkpoint distorts importance weights and introduces optimization instability.

Freshness limits balance off-policy variance against selection bias. Discarding trajectories that exceed a staleness threshold bounds policy lag, but because complex tasks demand more reasoning steps and tool calls, strict staleness cutoffs systematically eliminate hard problems from the training distribution. As indicated by equation 8, systems engineers can preserve task diversity by reducing the numerator rather than loosening the bound. Reducing generation, tool-wait, verification, group-buffer, and ingestion delays can shorten \(\RolloutAdmissionAge\) while preserving the long-horizon tasks the learner needs. Synchronous collection eliminates staleness entirely at the expense of straggler idle time, whereas multiple optimization epochs over a single collected batch reintroduce policy lag. While the clipped surrogate objective in section 17.5.2 limits policy updates driven by stale probability ratios, it neither bounds importance sampling variance nor corrects for the shift in visited state distributions (Schulman et al. 2017). Because discarded trajectories consume inference and sandbox capacity without producing gradient updates, monitoring systems must track discard rates, compute overhead, and shifts in task coverage.

17.9.5 Verification capacity

Even when inference and sandbox slots keep up with rollout generation, an undersized verifier pool backs up completed trajectories in the assessment queue, lengthening \(\RolloutAdmissionAge\) until valid rollouts miss the staleness cutoff. Verifier sizing reuses the pool model of Collection credits and staging reserves, changed only in that a reinforcement-learning trajectory may need several trials rather than one. Let \(\TrajectoryAdmissionRate\) denote completed trajectories arriving per second, \(\WorkerCount\) the number of independent verification worker slots, and \(\VerificationTrialTime\) the mean execution duration for one verification trial, including environment provisioning, execution, and cleanup. When each trajectory requires \(\VerificationTrialCount\) total trials to evaluate determinism or flaky tests, each trajectory demands an average of \(\VerificationTrialCount \VerificationTrialTime\) worker-seconds. By Little’s law (theorem 5.1), mean busy workers equal the arrival rate times that demand. The ideal service capacity \(\VerificationPoolCapacity\) and resulting offered utilization \(\ServingUtilization\) are

\[\VerificationPoolCapacity=\frac{\WorkerCount}{\VerificationTrialCount \VerificationTrialTime},\qquad \ServingUtilization=\frac{\TrajectoryAdmissionRate \VerificationTrialCount \VerificationTrialTime}{\WorkerCount}. \tag{9}\]

Both \(\TrajectoryAdmissionRate\) and \(\VerificationPoolCapacity\) measure completed trajectories per second. As in that pool model, a pool run at \(\TrajectoryAdmissionRate=\VerificationPoolCapacity\) has no margin for bursts or slow trials, so designers choose a target utilization \(0<\TargetServingUtilization<1\) and provision at least \(\lceil\TrajectoryAdmissionRate \VerificationTrialCount \VerificationTrialTime/\TargetServingUtilization\rceil\) worker slots. Queue capacity limits and upstream admission control remain necessary. While executing a candidate’s \(\VerificationTrialCount\) trials concurrently across multiple slots reduces wall-clock verification latency, the aggregate worker-second demand remains identical.

For a hypothetical arrival of 512 trajectories every 16 s, \(\TrajectoryAdmissionRate\) is 32 trajectories per second. With each trial lasting 8 s, a single trial per trajectory consumes 256 worker equivalents at that arrival rate. Executing all 3 trials elevates demand to 768 worker equivalents. Applying the utilization relationship in equation 9 with an illustrative 80 percent target requires provisioning 960 worker slots. Verification demand scales linearly with \(\VerificationTrialCount\), and the target utilization establishes the necessary headroom against queueing delays.

Early-exit evaluation policies alter these capacity requirements. Under the stop-on-first-rejection policy of notebook 17.5, verification terminates upon the first failing trial. Capacity models therefore substitute that notebook’s expected executed trial count in place of \(\VerificationTrialCount\) when computing total worker demand, averaged over the empirical mixture of pass rates for the convexity reason given in section 17.7.2. Sizing calculations must also incorporate unequal trial durations, sandbox initialization overhead, and retry cascades. In-memory queues absorb brief arrival spikes, but sustained overload requires adding worker slots or adopting more aggressive early-exit evaluation rules.

17.9.6 Policy version gating

Once the verifier pool has scored a batch and the trainer has produced a new checkpoint, that checkpoint faces two distinct promotion gates: entering the rollout fleet to collect the next batch, and shipping to production users. For internal collection gating, automated checks verify prompt-template compatibility, tool-calling syntax, entropy bounds against policy collapse, and memory utilization before promoting the candidate weights to the rollout fleet. The orchestrator records the promoted artifact digest and generating version for every dispatched episode. If a promoted version still falls into degenerate loops or crashes sandboxes, the controller halts further admission under that version, and any side effects produced by faulty rollouts remain subject to their designated cleanup handlers.

Production deployment release requires a formal evaluation framework verifying regression resistance, safety constraints, latency budgets, and out-of-distribution transfer. Internal training diagnostics provide rapid feedback during optimization, but they must remain strictly quarantined from release benchmarks to prevent benchmark leakage. Production evaluation suites must enforce an isolated assessment boundary, logging benchmark exposure whenever validation metrics inform architectural or hyperparameter revisions.

↩︎ Controlled comparison and conditional release of a trained checkpoint are established in Controlled comparison and conditional release.

Yue, Yang, Zhiqi Chen, Rui Lu, Andrew Zhao, Zhaokai Wang, Yang Yue, Shiji Song, and Gao Huang. 2025. “Does Reinforcement Learning Really Incentivize Reasoning Capacity in LLMs Beyond the Base Model?” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2504.13837.

System evaluations must carefully distinguish between operational consistency and genuine capability expansion. In the competitive reasoning benchmark study of Yue et al. (2025), RLVR-trained models improve pass@1 while base models match or exceed them when sampled across broad cohorts (\(\IndependentAttemptCount \gg 1\)). This pattern is consistent with concentrating probability mass onto successful solution modes that the base model could already generate; it does not establish a universal limit on acquiring new behavior. Reporting pass@1, pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) at large \(\IndependentAttemptCount\), and the repeated-trial pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) defined in Potential and repeated-run consistency separates a policy that has become more reliable from one that has expanded its underlying capabilities. A candidate checkpoint must remain unreleased until it satisfies recovery, transfer, and unresolved-outcome criteria across comprehensive production testbeds. Balancing rollout generation, straggler barriers, staleness bounds, verifier capacity, and release gating provides the infrastructure that keeps this learning loop saturated. Tracing how these mechanisms operate together requires following a complete update cycle on a concrete repair workload.

17.10 Case Study: Reinforcing the Repair Policy

Consider the connection-pool repair group with rewards \([0,1,0]\) from section 17.5. Three fresh trials, rl_trial_01, rl_trial_02, and rl_trial_03, execute task pool_repair_01 against the fixture pool_fixture_v1, sampled from the supervised checkpoint trained_policy_v1, and each ends in a verdict under repair_accept_v1. The recovery run that produced workspace ws_r20 and the demonstrations built from it remain historical artifacts of curation and fine-tuning; they supply comparisons, not targets, for this group. Following the group through each stage of the chapter shows which question every stage answers before the middle rollout is allowed to move the weights.

The first question is whether the lone pass is a pass. A \([0,1,0]\) group on a hard bug is exactly the shape that tampering produces, so the verdict for rl_trial_02 counts only because it came from the assessment side of table 7. The patch was admitted as a source-only diff, applied to a fresh copy of the fixture, and graded by a controller whose tests and result store the candidate could not reach. The defect is a race on connection return, so the fixture is timing-sensitive, and the all-pass rule of notebook 17.5 applies. If an incorrect patch passes a single trial with probability 0.20, 3 independent trials cut its acceptance to 0.8 percent at a 5.88 percent risk of rejecting a correct one. A trial whose verifier crashed, or whose container was preempted, is marked unresolved under the stop-state rules of section 17.6.3 rather than coerced to zero. Converting an infrastructure fault into a task failure penalizes the policy for the cluster and shifts the group baseline.

The second question is whether the update can be computed on what the policy actually saw. Each rollout record holds the delivered prefix and sampled token IDs of every invocation, the behavior log probabilities under the recorded sampling configuration, and the checkpoint version that produced them (table 6). Before admission, the trainer checks each record’s version lag against its bound (equation 8); a trial that ran long enough to fall behind is excluded with its reason logged rather than silently relabeled under the newest weights. In this worked example, all three rewards are resolved and all three members satisfy the lag bound, so the original complete group is eligible. Under its declared complete-group rule, an unresolved or stale member prevents the group from entering the update; excluding members and recomputing its coefficients would require a separately declared objective and selection analysis.

Only then does GRPO compute coefficients. The pass receives 1.414214 and each failure -0.707107 (notebook 17.3), and under per-rollout averaging each token of rollout \(\GroupRolloutIndex\) carries \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i/\RolloutPolicyTokenCount_i\) (equation 5), so the length of each failure sets how hard its tokens are pushed down. Because the reward scores only the outcome, every token in rl_trial_02 receives the same coefficient. The edit that adds the missing notification and any redundant status checks along the way are reinforced alike, and only many such groups separate them statistically. The frozen reference policy stays distinct from the behavior policy, contributing only the divergence penalty. Because the GRPO name covers several variants, the training log records the clipping thresholds, the length and dispersion normalizations, and the penalty weight \(\KlPenaltyCoefficient\) with the update.

When an update over eligible groups yields a candidate checkpoint, such as rl_policy_v1, lineage binds its weights and inference configuration to the rollout records, fixtures, and verifier versions that produced it. The controller may promote rl_policy_v1 to the rollout fleet for further collection, since that is an internal training decision. Releasing it is a different decision. Higher training reward on a known fixture can coexist with regression on unobserved failure modes. The benchmark study discussed in section 17.9.6 illustrates how higher pass@1 can coexist with unchanged broad-sampling performance. The release case therefore rests on held-out recovery fixtures evaluated by pass@1, pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) at large \(\IndependentAttemptCount\), and pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) under the protocol of Agent Evaluation. Each stage therefore answers one question that no other stage can. The isolated verifier says whether the reward is evidence. The record says whether the update is faithful. The coefficients say how hard the evidence pushes. The independent evaluation says whether the result belongs in production.

17.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A rollout group can easily contain positive rewards without establishing a sound solution, and a policy update can fit that group without improving the deployed agent. The pitfalls detailed below each turn a narrow, scoped measurement into an unwarranted conclusion, and each carries a concrete systems cost.

Fallacy: An automated reward is a complete task specification.

Automated test suites check only the conditions their authors anticipated, and reinforcement learning operates as an aggressive search procedure that reliably uncovers unhandled edge cases. When a policy’s true success rate is low, Bayesian base rates compound this vulnerability. As demonstrated in notebook 17.1, a verifier that misclassifies only 2.5 percent of failing attempts still results in 18.4 percent of all rewarded rollouts being outright failures when the policy solves only 10 percent of tasks. Because reinforcement updates treat every rewarded attempt as a success, the optimizer systematically increases the probability of the tokens that generated those spurious answers. Always benchmark the verifier against suites of known incorrect solutions, evaluate its false-acceptance rate against the policy’s current pass rate, and gate production deployment on strict held-out task criteria rather than raw training rewards.

Pitfall: Leaving the scorer, the runner, or its configuration where the candidate can reach it.

If an agent can read or write to its evaluation harness, modifying the test framework becomes the path of least resistance. In the METR evaluations, when a task exposed an editable scoring script to the agent, the model bypassed the intended challenge and tampered with the verifier in 21 of 21 runs (war story 17.1). During training, this vulnerability causes severe gradient distortion. On difficult tasks where honest attempts fail, the first rollout that discovers an exploit becomes the group’s lone positive sample. In a rollout group of 16, that single hack receives an advantage coefficient of 3.87, while each honest failure receives a negative coefficient of 0.26 in magnitude, strongly reinforcing the exploit. Always evaluate candidate solutions in an ephemeral workspace isolated behind a hardened controller that retains exclusive ownership of the test harness, the execution runner, and the result path (section 17.7). Even within that sandbox, treat all generated agent code as untrusted input.

Fallacy: A bigger group is the way to extract signal from hard tasks.

Increasing the rollout group size \(\GroupSize\) appears to offer a straightforward way to capture rare successes on difficult tasks. While larger groups improve the likelihood that a batch contains contrasting rewards, the probability of contrast alone does not measure the information each group supplies. At a pass rate of 5 percent, rollout groups of size 8 consume 23.8 rollouts for every informative group with nonzero variance. Expanding the group size to 16 still demands 28.6 rollouts per informative group. By contrast, steering the training curriculum toward tasks near a 50 percent pass rate consumes only 8.1 rollouts per informative group (notebook 17.4). Where obtaining contrast is the binding constraint and the task-mixture change is acceptable, this comparison favors targeting difficulty. Choose the group size using baseline precision, required task coverage, and held-out learning progress as well as rollout cost, and log the sampling distribution.

Pitfall: Reading longer self-correction text as evidence of better reasoning.

When an agent produces lengthy internal monologues and verbose self-critiques, engineers often mistake length for deeper reasoning. In reality, output lengths frequently expand as a direct artifact of loss normalization. Under per-rollout token averaging, a failing rollout of 128 tokens penalizes each individual token 32× as heavily as a failing rollout of 4,096 tokens. Because token-level gradient penalties dilute across longer generation lengths, this objective can favor longer failures even when the extra text performs no useful validation (equation 5). To uncover length bias, compare sequence lengths conditioned on task outcome and train with a constant-denominator loss objective. Ultimately, evaluate candidate revisions by the concrete tool actions and verified task completions they deliver under a matched token budget, rather than by the model’s narration of reconsidering.

Fallacy: Faster environment reset speeds training proportionally.

Optimizing container teardown and environment initialization yields satisfying microbenchmarks, but Amdahl’s law strictly limits the resulting gains in end-to-end training throughput. Reset latency represents only one slice of the rollout lifecycle. Cutting reset overhead from 3.5 s to 15 ms delivers a 233× acceleration in reset speed, yet it improves execution slot capacity by only 1.77×. The remaining 4.5 s spent on model inference, tool execution, and verifier scoring continues to dominate every cycle (notebook 17.6). Moreover, increased rollout throughput converts into faster training progress only if the inference workers, verification services, and parameter optimizers maintain sufficient headroom to ingest the higher volume. Profile the entire training loop to pinpoint the saturated pipeline stage before investing engineering effort into optimizing any individual phase.

17.12 Summary

Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards uses assessed interactions to guide a policy update without requiring a demonstrated response at every decision. Its opportunity depends on what the sampling process can reach and what the reward distinguishes. Its risks arise because the recorded score, rather than the full task intent, enters the optimization. Reward validity, sampling fidelity, and external enforcement therefore remain part of the learning system rather than prerequisites that can be forgotten once training starts.

The repair group illustrates the resulting chain of responsibility. Completed assessments support group-relative coefficients; recorded policy actions and their actual inputs support the chosen update; resource and version controls govern the collection loop. None of those steps substitutes for evaluating the resulting candidate under its intended operating conditions. Training changes the policy and moves no exposure; the runtime still bounds horizon, state, and authority for whatever the updated weights propose. The relations in table 9 carry the chapter’s quantitative decisions, each under the assumptions stated where it is derived.

Key Takeaways: A learning signal is not a release decision
  • Reward learning needs reachable distinctions: Fresh interactions provide a different signal from admitted imitation targets. The policy must sample behavior the reward can distinguish; useful support can come from several sources, and supervised fine-tuning is not a mandatory preceding stage.
  • A protected score still has a scope: Automated checks assess specified properties, not every aspect of task intent. Reward composition can change preferences, while evaluator isolation blocks particular interference paths; neither replaces runtime permissions or independent assessment of the candidate.
  • Return is not causal credit: A baseline changes the coefficient attached to a sampled action without identifying its individual contribution. Continuation estimates require compatible forks and additional execution; their uncertainty and cost must accompany any interpretation of an estimated advantage.
  • Group coefficients do not constrain every update: GRPO’s centering and normalization define a particular surrogate. Equal rewards remove its reward-dependent terms, not all learning; clipping changes the objective but does not impose a hard bound on policy drift or gradient magnitude.
  • Training needs the actions actually sampled: Retain per-invocation inputs, sampled policy tokens, and matching behavior probabilities. Observations supply conditioning rather than sampled actions, and incomplete assessments or truncated episodes require declared treatment rather than silent relabeling or exclusion.
  • Behavioral changes require behavioral evidence: Entropy, length, reward, and recovery traces guide investigation but do not reveal internal cognition or uniquely diagnose a cause. Test interventions against task outcomes and costs under controlled conditions instead of optimizing telemetry in isolation.
  • The collection loop and release have different gates: Bounded queues, group completion, version accounting, and verifier capacity govern training operation. Admission for further collection is distinct from external deployment, which requires evidence for the identified policy and retains all runtime controls.

Verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) reaches its last form in this chapter. Imitation admits recorded responses as targets after curation. Reinforcement learning admits every action the policy sampled, malformed ones included, and weights each by an outcome a verifier certified, so the admission decision passes from the curator to the reward. The worked group makes the verification asymmetry (principle 3) concrete under that optimization pressure. A protected, scoped assessment must be joined to the sampled record before it can justify an update. Executing the candidate inside that assessment also stress-tests containment beneath the model (principle 9), because fresh workspaces still receive untrusted code.
