The same asymmetry governs both ends of the loop. A learned score may shape an update, while the reward and the release decision rest on protected checks wherever such checks exist. Neither the learning signal nor an improved evaluation result moves authority into the weights; invariant closure (principle 1) continues to govern the effects of each proposal.

17.13 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The problem this chapter manages began as a question about games. Minsky (1961) asked how a learning system that sees only whether a long strategy won or lost can distribute credit among the many decisions that produced the result, and he named it the credit-assignment problem. Two answers followed that still organize the field. Temporal-difference learning predicted later outcomes from earlier ones, so each step could be scored against the next prediction rather than the final result (Sutton 1988). The REINFORCE family instead differentiated expected reward directly through the probabilities of sampled actions and subtracted a reinforcement baseline to tame the variance (Williams 1992). The policy gradient theorem showed that such estimates remain valid with function approximation (Sutton et al. 1999), and the analysis of baselines as control variates explained why some reduce variance more than others (Greensmith et al. 2004).

Sutton, Richard S. 1988. “Learning to Predict by the Methods of Temporal Differences.” Machine Learning 3 (1): 9–44. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00115009.
Sutton, Richard S., David A. McAllester, Satinder P. Singh, and Yishay Mansour. 1999. “Policy Gradient Methods for Reinforcement Learning with Function Approximation.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 12 (NIPS 1999).
Greensmith, Evan, Peter L. Bartlett, and Jonathan Baxter. 2004. “Variance Reduction Techniques for Gradient Estimates in Reinforcement Learning.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 5: 1471–530.
Ho, Qirong, James Cipar, Henggang Cui, Seunghak Lee, Jin Kyu Kim, Phillip B. Gibbons, Garth A. Gibson, Greg Ganger, and Eric P. Xing. 2013. “More Effective Distributed ML via a Stale Synchronous Parallel Parameter Server.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 26.
Mnih, Volodymyr, Adrià Puigdomènech Badia, Mehdi Mirza, Alex Graves, Timothy Lillicrap, Tim Harley, David Silver, and Koray Kavukcuoglu. 2016. “Asynchronous Methods for Deep Reinforcement Learning.” Proceedings of the 33rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 1928–37.

Scaling policy gradients to neural networks exposed instability rather than bias. Trust region policy optimization bounded each update’s divergence from the policy that collected the data (Schulman et al. 2015). Generalized advantage estimation made the bias-variance trade in the baseline explicit (Schulman et al. 2016). Proximal policy optimization replaced the trust region with the cheaper clipped objective that GRPO and many later methods inherit (Schulman et al. 2017). The systems problem arrived in the same years. Stale-synchronous parameter servers bounded how old a worker’s parameters could be (Ho et al. 2013), asynchronous actor-learners kept every actor busy at the price of that lag (Mnih et al. 2016), and IMPALA separated acting from learning with an explicit off-policy correction (Espeholt et al. 2018), a split that agent rollout fleets resemble.

Language models then turned the reward itself into the hard part. Learning a reward model from human comparisons made preferences optimizable (Christiano et al. 2017; Ouyang et al. 2022), and measuring what happens when a policy is optimized against such a proxy showed a held-out gold score eventually declining while the proxy score keeps rising (Gao et al. 2023). Executable checks offered a sturdier signal: unit tests became the reward for code generation (Le et al. 2022), outcome and process supervision were compared on checkable reasoning (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024), and the name reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards was attached to training against such checks (Lambert et al. 2024). On the optimizer side, sampled baselines that score each output against its siblings (Kool et al. 2019; Ahmadian et al. 2024) led to group-relative policy optimization (Shao et al. 2024). The same years documented the cost of an imperfect reward, from the early catalog of specification problems (Amodei et al. 2016; Krakovna et al. 2020) to a formal definition of reward hacking (Skalse et al. 2022). The defense returned to the protection literature, where confinement (Lampson 1973) and complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975) describe the verifier boundary in terms that predate learned policies entirely. For further reading, six works trace that lineage from classical learning theory to agent training:

Christiano, P. F., J. Leike, T. B. Brown, M. Martic, S. Legg, and D. Amodei. 2017. “Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 30: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2017, December 4-9, 2017, Long Beach, CA, USA, edited by Isabelle Guyon, Ulrike von Luxburg, Samy Bengio, Hanna M. Wallach, Rob Fergus, S. V. N. Vishwanathan, and Roman Garnett. Curran Associates.
Ouyang, L., J. Wu, X. Jiang, D. Almeida, C. L. Wainwright, P. Mishkin, C. Zhang, et al. 2022. “Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 27730–44. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2011.
Gao, Leo, John Schulman, and Jacob Hilton. 2023. “Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 10835–66.
Uesato, Jonathan, Nate Kushman, Ramana Kumar, Francis Song, Noah Siegel, Lisa Wang, Antonia Creswell, Geoffrey Irving, and Irina Higgins. 2022. “Solving Math Word Problems with Process- and Outcome-Based Feedback.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2211.14275, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2211.14275.
Lightman, Hunter, Vineet Kosaraju, Yura Burda, Harri Edwards, Bowen Baker, Teddy Lee, Jan Leike, John Schulman, Ilya Sutskever, and Karl Cobbe. 2024. “Let’s Verify Step by Step.” The Twelfth International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Lambert, Nathan, Jacob Morrison, Valentina Pyatkin, Shengyi Huang, Hamish Ivison, Faeze Brahman, Lester James V. Miranda, et al. 2024. “Tülu 3: Pushing Frontiers in Open Language Model Post-Training.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2411.15124.
Shao, Zhihong, Peiyi Wang, Qihao Zhu, et al. 2024. “DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning in Open Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2402.03300, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2402.03300.
Amodei, D., C. Olah, J. Steinhardt, P. Christiano, J. Schulman, and D. Mané. 2016. “Concrete Problems in AI Safety.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1606.06565, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1606.06565.
Krakovna, Victoria, Jonathan Uesato, Vladimir Mikulik, Matthew Rahtz, Tom McGrath, Celestine Mendler-Dünner, Kyle Haslett, et al. 2020. “Specification Gaming: The Flip Side of Specification Design.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2011.08827.
Lampson, Butler W. 1973. “A Note on the Confinement Problem.” Communications of the ACM 16 (10): 613–15.
  • Sutton and Barto (2018) is the standard text on returns, value functions, temporal-difference learning, and policy gradients. It supplies the vocabulary every section of this chapter assumes.
  • Williams (1992) introduces the REINFORCE estimator and the reinforcement baseline. Reading it shows how little the core update has changed since.
  • Schulman et al. (2017) presents the clipped objective, read together with Schulman et al. (2016) for advantage estimation. The pair explains what GRPO keeps and what it removes.
  • Espeholt et al. (2018) is the reference design for separating actors from learners with a bounded, corrected lag. Its staleness problem is the one agent rollouts make worse.
  • Skalse et al. (2022) gives a precise definition of when a proxy reward can be hacked. It turns the informal worry of this chapter into a property a reward designer can reason about.
  • Saltzer and Schroeder (1975) states the protection principles that verifier isolation applies. Reading it shows why an instruction to the policy is never a substitute for mediation of every route to the verdict.
Sutton, Richard S., and Andrew G. Barto. 2018. Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction. 2nd ed. MIT Press.
Williams, Ronald J. 1992. “Simple Statistical Gradient-Following Algorithms for Connectionist Reinforcement Learning.” Machine Learning 8 (3–4): 229–56. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00992696.
Skalse, Joar, Nikolaus H. R. Howe, Dmitrii Krasheninnikov, and David Krueger. 2022. “Defining and Characterizing Reward Gaming.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 9460–71.
Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.

17.14 Exercises

Every given below is a hypothetical problem input rather than a measurement of a real system. Each problem ends in a decision, and the calculation exists to justify it.

  1. False accepts early and late in training (Basic). A repair-task verifier accepts every criterion success (\(\VerifierTrueAcceptanceRate=1\)) and a fraction \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate=0.02\) of criterion failures. At the start of training the policy passes the reference criterion with probability \(\ValidCandidateRate=0.04\); after several hundred updates it passes with \(\ValidCandidateRate=0.40\).

    1. Use equation 1 to compute the false-accept share of rewarded rollouts at both pass rates.
    2. Find the largest \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) that keeps the share at or below \(0.10\) when \(\ValidCandidateRate=0.04\).
    3. The team can afford one verifier-hardening effort, either before training starts or after the pass rate has risen. Decide when to spend it and justify the choice from parts (a) and (b).

  2. Keeping the pass above the failure (Basic). A proposed terminal score is \(\TrajectoryReward=\CorrectnessReward+\FormatRewardWeight\FormatComplianceScore-\LengthPenaltyWeight\text{len}\) with \(\FormatRewardWeight=0.4\), \(\LengthPenaltyWeight=0.7\), and \(\text{len}=\RolloutPolicyTokenCount/\GeneratedTokenCap\) for an enforced cap \(\GeneratedTokenCap=8{,}000\) generated tokens.

    1. Score a pass that uses \(7{,}200\) tokens with \(\FormatComplianceScore=0.5\), and a failure that uses \(400\) tokens with \(\FormatComplianceScore=1\). State whether this pair keeps the intended order.
    2. Compute the lowest possible pass score and the highest possible failure score over the stated ranges, and state whether every observed pass outranks every observed failure.
    3. Holding \(\FormatRewardWeight=0.4\), find the range of \(\LengthPenaltyWeight\) that satisfies the sufficient ordering condition, and explain why part (a) alone was not evidence that the original weights were safe.

  3. Sizing an all-pass rule (Basic). A timing-sensitive fixture passes a fixed incorrect patch with per-trial probability \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability=0.30\) and a fixed correct patch with \(\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability=0.95\). Trials are independent, acceptance requires \(\VerificationTrialCount\) total passes, and verification stops at the first rejection.

    1. Find the smallest \(\VerificationTrialCount\) that brings an incorrect patch’s acceptance probability below \(0.01\).
    2. At that \(\VerificationTrialCount\), compute the false-rejection probability for the correct patch and the expected number of trials for each patch.
    3. A lone false pass on a hard task receives the largest group coefficient. Compare adding trials with repairing the fixture’s timing sensitivity, and state which the loop should do first.

  4. A mixed task pool and its silent groups (Intermediate). An update samples groups of \(\GroupSize=8\) over \(256\) tasks. Half the tasks belong to a family the policy passes with \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.02\); the other half to a family it passes with \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.60\). Runs are independent and rewards are binary.

    1. Compute \(\GroupContrastProbability\) for each family, the expected number of informative groups per update, and the rollouts spent per informative group across the whole pool.
    2. Compute \(\GroupContrastProbability\) from the pool-average pass rate instead, and explain the size of the error.
    3. For the hard family, compute the coefficient a lone pass receives with \(\AdvantageStabilizer\to0\). A colleague proposes dropping the hard family to cut the rollout bill. Critique the proposal, naming what it does to the training distribution, and propose an alternative that raises the hard family’s contrast without discarding it.

  5. A continuation-sampling budget (Intermediate). A repair trajectory runs \(\TurnHorizon=40\) turns of \(\TurnGeneratedTokenCount=400\) generated tokens. The team may spend at most \(30\) times the base trajectory’s generated tokens on credit estimation, including the base. Continuations are independent binary returns and are not recursively forked.

    1. Compute the generation multiple for exhaustive forking with \(\ContinuationCount=16\) continuations per boundary, and for forks after turns \(10\), \(20\), and \(30\) only.
    2. Find the \(\ContinuationCount\) that brings the per-fork standard-error bound to \(0.05\), and the multiple that \(\ContinuationCount\) costs at the three chosen forks.
    3. Find the largest \(\ContinuationCount\) the budget allows at those three forks and its standard-error bound. Treat a difference between two fork values as resolved only if it is at least twice the bound on the standard error of the difference, \(\sqrt2/(2\sqrt \ContinuationCount)\). Decide whether this schedule can resolve a value difference of \(0.20\), and if it cannot, redesign the schedule within the same budget.

  6. Reset for a short-horizon support agent (Intermediate, second-lighthouse transfer). Apply the slot model of equation 6 to training the support agent on refund tasks, where a deterministic check compares the issued refund with the recorded policy. An episode has \(\TurnHorizon=4\) decisions with \(\ModelTime=0.6\) s of model service and \(\ToolTime=0.1\) s of record lookup each, then \(\RolloutAssessmentTime=0.2\) s of assessment. Restoring the customer record and refund history from a cold fixture takes \(\EnvironmentResetTime=6.0\) s; a snapshot restore takes \(0.3\) s. The pool has \(64\) slots, and the shared model service can sustain at most \(40{,}000\) episodes per hour.

    1. Compute the cycle time and the reset share of the cycle for both resets, and the idealized pool capacity in episodes per hour.
    2. Compute the capacity gain that the shared model service actually permits, and decide whether the snapshot work or added model-service capacity should come next.
    3. In the chapter’s repair-pool example, cold reset was under half of the cycle. Compare this lighthouse’s reset share, explain which term of equation 6 makes reset the lever here, and name the state a local snapshot does not restore if a refund reached an external payment service.

  7. Staleness bounds and verifier provisioning (Advanced). A repair-agent trainer steps every \(\TrainingStepTime=40\) s and admits a record only if \(\FiniteChange\text{ver}\le2\). Of the rollouts, a fraction \(0.70\) take \(30\) s from first token to admission, \(0.25\) take \(90\) s, and \(0.05\) take \(200\) s. Verification receives \(256\) completed trajectories per training step. Every trajectory needs up to \(\VerificationTrialCount=3\) verification trials of \(\VerificationTrialTime=10\) s, and the pool targets \(\TargetServingUtilization=0.8\).

    1. Compute \(\FiniteChange\text{ver}\) for each duration class and the fraction of rollouts excluded. Explain why the excluded fraction understates the damage to hard tasks.
    2. Find the step time above which the \(200\) s rollouts are admitted, and the duration below which a rollout is admitted at the current step time. Compare slowing the trainer, capping rollout duration, and shortening \(\FiniteChange \RolloutDuration\).
    3. Size the verifier pool when all three trials execute. Then size it under stop-on-first-rejection when a fraction \(0.60\) of candidates are incorrect with \(\IncorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability=0.10\) and the rest are correct with \(\CorrectCandidateTrialPassProbability=0.97\).
    4. Size the stop-on-first-rejection pool again by substituting the population’s mean pass probability into every power, and state the consequence of provisioning from that number.

  8. Diagnosing a lengthening policy (Advanced, critique and redesign). During GRPO training with per-rollout token averaging, the mean length of failing repair rollouts grows from \(2{,}000\) to \(6{,}000\) generated tokens while acceptance stays flat. The team proposes adding \(\LengthPenaltyWeight\text{len}\) with \(\LengthPenaltyWeight=0.4\) and \(\FormatRewardWeight=0.2\), where \(\text{len}=\RolloutPolicyTokenCount/4{,}000\) is normalized by a nominal cap the runtime does not enforce.

    1. Using equation 5, compute the ratio of per-token penalties on a \(2{,}000\)-token failure and a \(6{,}000\)-token failure that share the same \(\GroupRelativeAdvantage_i\).
    2. Score a \(9{,}000\)-token pass with \(\FormatComplianceScore=0\) and a \(500\)-token failure with \(\FormatComplianceScore=1\) under the proposal. Find the pass length beyond which a pass with \(\FormatComplianceScore=0\) scores below the best-scoring failure.
    3. Critique the proposal and redesign the response. The redesign should state which diagnostic runs before any penalty is added, what the runtime must enforce, and which condition any length term that survives must satisfy.

What’s Next: From a measured single agent to coordination
Splitting a task across several agents is justified only when the benefits of decomposition exceed its coordination costs. Every mechanism so far, from the harness to this chapter’s repair group, governs one trajectory, and the connection-pool race that group repaired fits in one controller’s context. The repair agent’s next assignment is wider: migrating an authentication module across a repository, a change that reaches every caller of its interface and tempts a split. Part V provides ways to curate learning material, update a policy, and test the result, but no system must complete all three stages before asking whether to delegate. Multi-Agent Coordination compares a coordinated design with a capable single-agent baseline that holds the same parallel tools and a comparable resource budget. Delegation can separate work and context, but it also introduces shared-state conflicts, correlated mistakes, and obligations that cross agent boundaries, and the comparison must price those costs while preserving every runtime control already required for one agent.
